### I / 一

===Original===
Then I went to Oxford, and when the war broke out I joined the Navy. But just before the revolution, Admiral Butt, who had gone on a special mission to Russia, applied for my “Anglo-Russian” services.

I still remember very vividly the morning following on my arrival in Petrograd, when I had to meet the Admiral for the first time at the British Embassy. I ascended the broad staircase with its worn red carpet to the Chancery. Very perfect young men, very perfectly dressed, were conversing in very perfect intonations about love among monkeys. It struck me as delightfully human for diplomats. When I descended, the Admiral had not yet arrived. I talked to Yuri, the hall-porter, a clean-shaven individual of uncertain nationality, violently pro-British, and speaking several languages all very badly. Every now and then the great heavy door would open﻿—it was snowing heavily outside﻿—and some man or woman would come in and inquire if this was the Military District Staff. “It is the British Embassy,” replied Yuri proudly. And he explained the error. The Military District Staff was 4 Palace Square; the Embassy 4 Palace Quay. In peacetime people did come in occasionally and inquire if this was the Military District Staff; but since war had been declared they seemed to be doing little else. I pondered over the possibility of Yuri, unable one day to withstand the increasing pressure of inquiries, going mad and holding forth on this subject to his brother inmates henceforth indefinitely in a lunatic asylum.

===Chinese===
后来我上了牛津（Oxford）大学，战争一爆发便参加了海军。可就在革命前夕，因特别使命赴俄的巴特（Butt）海军上将，申请调用我去，要的就是我这份「英俄」之才。

我至今清清楚楚记得初到彼得格勒（Petrograd）后的那个早晨——那天我得去英国大使馆，头一回拜见海军上将。我踏上铺着磨旧红地毯的宽大楼梯，上到使馆办公处（Chancery）。几位十分完美的年轻人，衣着十分完美，正用十分完美的语调，谈论猴子之间的爱情。在我看来，这在外交官身上，可真是人情味十足，可爱得很。我下楼时，海军上将还没有到。我便同门房尤里（Yuri）攀谈起来。这人脸刮得精光，国籍成谜，狂热亲英，会讲好几国语言，可惜每一国都讲得很糟。那扇沉重的大门隔三差五就打开一回——外面大雪下得正紧——总有男人或女人走进来，问这里是不是军区参谋部（Military District Staff）。「这里是英国大使馆。」尤里自豪地答道，并且把误会解释一番：军区参谋部在宫殿广场（Palace Square）4号；大使馆在宫殿滨河街（Palace Quay）4号。和平年月里，偶尔也有人进来问这里是不是军区参谋部；可自打宣战以来，人们仿佛别的什么都不干了，专干这一件事。我不禁琢磨起一种可能：有朝一日，尤里抵不住与日俱增的问讯重压，发了疯，从此在疯人院里对着同院的病友，就这个题目无休无止地大发议论。

===Original===
Helping me on with my coat, Yuri was suddenly seized by a strange panic. He dropped the coat on the floor and dashed to the door. I followed him, thinking it was the revolution. I was rewarded for my exertion. The Ambassador’s car drove up, and sitting in it were Sir George Buchanan and the French Ambassador. Yuri sprang up and pulling off his cap opened the door of the vehicle and stood still in a paroxysm of reverence and awe. But the two great men within continued talking, the Frenchman in that agitated, agile manner that Frenchmen have, the Englishman with a fine superiority of distinction. The Ambassadors of the two friendly powers sat talking, evidently unaware that they had arrived. Yuri held the door open, still bareheaded, the incarnation of servility and devotion. Then they entered. Yuri made a dash for Sir George’s feet, and began hastily to unbuckle his felt goloshes, while the great diplomat with his fur collar still up to his temples and his round fur cap cocked over one ear stood panting in his great fur coat. I had an absurd idea that something great must be happening on the political horizon.

Finally the Admiral arrived. He was a tall, imperious figure. His movements were powerful and sweeping. He had the air of a man engaged in winning the war while everybody else about him was obstructing him in his patriotic task. His voice was the voice of such a man. His look seemed specially selected to match his voice. That war-winning quality was clearly manifest in his personality, but his actual work towards that end was all very obscure.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===
尤里帮我穿大衣时，忽然被一阵莫名的惊慌攫住。他把大衣往地上一撂，直奔门口而去。我以为革命来了，便跟着他跑了过去。我这一趟没有白跑。大使的汽车驶到门前，车里坐着乔治·布坎南爵士（Sir George Buchanan）和法国大使。尤里一跃而起，一把拽下帽子，拉开车门，随即一动不动地伫立着，崇敬与惶恐到了痉挛的地步。可车内两位大人物谈兴正浓：法国人用的是法国人特有的那种亢奋敏捷的做派，英国人则是一派优雅超然的气度。两个友邦的大使坐在车里谈个不停，分明没有觉察自己已经到了。尤里扶着车门，仍旧光着头，活脱脱是卑屈与忠心的化身。随后他们走了进去。尤里一个箭步扑到乔治爵士脚前，急急忙忙去解他毡套鞋的搭扣；那位大外交官毛皮领子还直竖到太阳穴，圆毛皮帽歪扣在一只耳朵上，裹在那件大皮大衣里直喘气。我心里冒出一个荒唐的念头：政治地平线上必定正有什么大事发生。

海军上将终于到了。他身材高大，气派专断，一举一动都威猛豪迈。他浑身上下透着一股劲头：这场战争全靠他一人去打赢，而他身边的每一个人都在给他的爱国大业使绊子。他的嗓音正是这样一个人的嗓音，他的目光又仿佛是特意挑选来与嗓音相配的。那种「打赢战争」的气质在他身上展露无遗，至于他为达此目的究竟做着什么实际工作，那就十分晦暗不明了……

===Original===
Then one morning, as I was about to cross the Troitski Bridge to meet the Admiral, I was stopped by the police and was compelled to go home and change into uniform. When I returned the revolution had already broken out. The Admiral had just witnessed the sacking of the Arsenal by a disorderly crowd. Regiment after regiment was going over to the revolution. Solitary shots, and now and then machine-gun fire, were heard from various quarters of the city. The Admiral and I stood at the window and watched. Lorry after lorry packed with armed soldiery and workmen, some lying in a “ready” attitude along the mudguards, went past us in a kind of wild and dazzling joy ride, waving red flags and revolutionary banners to shouts of “Hurrah!” from the crowds in the street. The Admiral stood with his hands folded on the windowsill, unable to withhold his enthusiasm. It was a clear, bright day, I think, and very cold.

===Chinese===
后来一个早晨，我正要穿过特罗伊茨基桥（Troitski Bridge）去见海军上将，被警察拦下，只得回家换上军装。待我再出门，革命已经爆发。海军上将刚刚亲眼目睹一群乱民洗劫了兵工厂。一个团又一个团的士兵倒向革命。零星的枪声，间或夹着机关枪声，从城中四面八方传来。海军上将和我站在窗前观望。一辆接一辆的卡车载满武装的士兵和工人，有的摆出「预备」姿势趴在挡泥板上，以一种狂野而炫目的兜风架势从我们面前驶过，车上的人挥着红旗和革命的横幅，迎着街上人群「乌拉！」「乌拉！」的欢呼。海军上将双手交叠搭在窗台上，按捺不住心头的兴奋。我想，那是个晴朗明媚的日子，而且很冷。

===Original===
That evening following the outbreak of the revolution was vividly impressed upon my memory. During the day I had listened to innumerable speeches, some of a Liberal loftiness; others of a menacingly proletarian character, threatening death to capital and revolution to the world at large. There was a tendency to flamboyant extravagance and exaggeration. “Down with Armies and Navies!” shouted one speaker hysterically. “Down with militarism! Through red terror to peace, freedom and brotherhood!” There were placards and banners and processions. “Land and Liberty!” was a popular watchword. Red was the dominant colour, and the opening bars of the Marseillaise were a kind of recurring leitmotif in the tumult. Crossing a bridge I passed a company of soldiers newly revolted. They marched alert and joyous to the sound of some old familiar marching song till they came to the words “for the Czar.” Having sung these words they stopped somewhat abruptly and perplexed. “*How* for the Czar?” one of them asked. “*How* for the Czar?” they repeated, looking at each other sheepishly. Then they marched on without singing. There were peasants who did not know the word “revolution” and thought it was a woman who would supersede the Czar. Others wanted a republic with a czar. And there were others still who interpreted the word republic as “rieszshpublicoo,” thinking that it meant “cut up the public.” In the Troitski Square I was stopped by a young enthusiastic Russian officer who, attracted by my British uniform, spoke to me in English, his eyes glittering with excitement. “Sir,” he said, “you now will have more vigorous Allies.” And then in the Nevski I passed a procession of Anarchists who are regarded by the Bolsheviks with about the same degree of unmitigated horror as the Bolsheviks are regarded by the *Morning Post*. They marched with a gruesome look about their faces, bearing their horrible colours of black, crested with a human skull and crossbones.

And somewhat later in the day I sat at dinner with Zina’s people on the Petrograd side, and the presence of a score of students, male and female, an engineer, a lawyer, and a journalist or two, all of that revolutionary intelligentsia, probably accounted for the Liberal atmosphere that prevailed. Yesterday they had been revolutionaries; today they were contented Liberals, hailing Lvov and Miliukov as the heroes of the day. The engineer drank to the future: “The old world is dead: long live the new world!”

===Chinese===
革命爆发后的那个夜晚，在我的记忆里烙下了鲜活的印迹。白天我听了数不清的演说：有的是自由派的高腔远调，有的带着咄咄逼人的无产阶级色彩，扬言要叫资本死，要革命闹遍全世界。字里行间有一种浮夸炫饰、言过其实的倾向。「打倒陆军和海军！」一位演讲者歇斯底里地喊道。「打倒军国主义！通过红色恐怖，走向和平、自由、博爱！」满眼是标语、横幅和游行队伍。「土地与自由」是一句风行的口号。红色是压倒一切的色调，《马赛曲》（Marseillaise）开头的几小节，成了这片喧嚣中反复回响的主导旋律。过一座桥时，我迎面遇上一小队新近哗变的士兵。他们伴着一支熟悉的老进行曲行进，机敏而欢快，一直唱到「为了沙皇」那句词。这句一唱完，他们便有些突兀地停了下来，一脸茫然。「*怎么*个『为了沙皇』法？」其中一个问道。「*怎么*个『为了沙皇』法？」他们面面相觑，讪讪地重复。随后便不再唱，继续行军。有庄稼汉不知道「革命」这个词，还当它是个女人，要来接沙皇的班。有人想要一个留着沙皇的共和国。更有人把「共和国」这个词念成「雷什普布利库」（rieszshpublicoo），以为意思是「把公众剁碎」。在特罗伊茨基广场（Troitski Square），一位热情的俄国青年军官拦住了我；他被我这身英国军装吸引，对我讲起英语来，双眼兴奋得发亮。「先生，」他说，「你们如今要有一批更强有力的盟友了。」接着在涅夫斯基大街（Nevski），我又遇上一队无政府主义者的游行——布尔什维克（Bolsheviks）看待他们的那种毫不打折扣的恐惧，同《晨邮报》（Morning Post）看待布尔什维克的恐惧不相上下。他们脸上带着阴森可怖的神情行进，扛着那面骇人的黑旗，旗首顶着一颗人的骷髅和交叉的白骨。

当天晚上再晚些时候，我在彼得格勒区（Petrograd side）季娜（Zina）家里吃饭；席间有二十来个男女学生、一位工程师、一位律师，还有一两个记者，全属于革命知识界——满座的自由派气氛，多半就是这么来的。昨天他们还是革命者；今天已经心满意足地当起自由派，把李沃夫（Lvov）和米留科夫（Miliukov）奉为当日的英雄。工程师为未来干杯：「旧世界死了：新世界万岁！」

===Original===
The two ancient grandfathers were much too old and feeble to intervene on behalf of the old order of things; they had exhausted their Liberal aspirations with the liberation of the serfs in 1861 and could not see what in the world more anybody wanted. Zina’s father, underpaid and ill as he was, had lost forever the hope of seeing better days, and failed to see how the revolution could affect his own position. Nikolai Vasilievich was still keeping him so far. These Liberals interpreted the revolution as a protest against the pro-German tendency at court, and as an attempt to get into line with the Western Democracies in this hitherto unconvincing struggle against militarism and autocracy. The news was rumoured that the Czar had abdicated. Again, it was said that a section of the court had been planning a revolution to depose the Emperor and substitute his brother Michael in order to carry on the war more vigorously; and that the people’s revolution had preceded it by two days. Some monarchists now wanted to put down the revolution in order to carry on the war; other monarchists wanted to put down the war in order to put down the revolution; and still other monarchists wanted to put down the revolution and did not care a hang about the war. The Liberals wanted the revolution to carry on the war; the Czar wanted to put down the revolution; the Socialists and workmen wanted to put down the war and to put down the Czar; and the soldiers and sailors wanted to put down their officers. The Liberal gathering drank to Russia’s Allies; and then Uncle Kostia, obviously moved by the great event, rose and said in a slow, melodious voice:

===Chinese===
两位老祖父年纪太大、身子太弱，再也无力出面维护旧秩序了；他们那点自由派的抱负，早在一八六一年解放农奴那阵就已用尽，实在想不明白世人到底还想要什么。季娜的父亲薪水微薄，又疾病缠身，早已永远断了过上好日子的指望，也看不出革命能给他自己的处境带来什么变化——到那时为止，一直是尼古拉·瓦西里耶维奇（Nikolai Vasilievich）在养活他。这些自由派把革命解释为对宫廷亲德倾向的抗议，解释为在这场迄今难服人心的反军国主义、反专制斗争中同西方民主国家看齐的一种努力。有传闻说，沙皇已经退位。又有人说，宫廷里有一派人原也密谋着一场革命，要废黜皇帝，让他弟弟米哈伊尔（Michael）取而代之，好把仗打得更起劲，却不料人民的革命抢在了前头两天。于是，一些保皇党人想镇压革命，好把战争打下去；另一些保皇党人想结束战争，好镇压革命；还有一些保皇党人想镇压革命，至于战争，则全不放在心上。自由派想借着革命把战争打下去；沙皇想镇压革命；社会党人和工人想结束战争、打倒沙皇；士兵和水兵想打倒他们的长官。这场自由派的聚会为俄国的盟友们举了杯；随后，科斯佳叔叔（Uncle Kostia）显然被这桩大事打动了，站起身来，用缓慢而悦耳的嗓音说道：

===Original===
“We will not talk about or criticize the past. We will carry it gently into the depths of the garden and bury it there among the flowers. And then, carefully, we will look into the cradle and nurse very tenderly the slumbering future.﻿ ﻿…”

This attitude, we all felt, was befitting to the great bloodless revolution.

===Chinese===
「过去的事，我们不谈，也不指摘。我们要轻轻地把它捧起来，送进花园深处，葬在那里的花丛中间。然后，我们再小心翼翼地凑近摇篮，无比温柔地守护那沉睡中的未来……」

我们大家都觉得，这样的态度，正与这场伟大的不流血的革命相称。

### II / 二

===Original===
I remember the excitement of it all. Uncle Kostia, it appeared, had risen earlier that day on account of the revolution; and after dinner, still in his dressing-gown and slippers, he paced the floor quicker than was his custom, and, contrary to his practice, discoursed at great length. He held that history was moving at an unheard-of pace, and he complained that it was indeed difficult for him, a historian, to keep pace with it. The revolution had overtaken Uncle Kostia as he was still tackling the age of Anne.

===Chinese===
我记得这一切有多叫人兴奋。据说，科斯佳叔叔那天因为革命，起得比平时早了些；饭后，他还穿着晨衣、趿着拖鞋，在地板上踱来踱去，步子比往常快，而且一反常态，大发了一通议论。他宣称，历史正以闻所未闻的速度向前飞奔，又抱怨说，他这个当历史学家的，要跟上去实在是难为其难。革命追上科斯佳叔叔的时候，他还在埋头攻克安妮女王时代（the age of Anne）呢。

===Original===
From Zina’s house I remember walking to the Bursanovs in the Mohovaya. I passed the sombre silhouettes of the snow-covered barges frozen on the Neva. It was dark now and the crowds in the streets were more tumultuous. Soldiers and civilians alike walked aimlessly, rifle slung over the shoulder. Several wine-cellars had been broken into; there were drunkards in the streets; but anyhow, all seemed drunk with the revolution. Shots were heard every now and then, mostly fired in the air, while the law courts had gone up in flames. The revolution, it was felt, had been established.

===Chinese===
记得我从季娜家出来，步行到莫霍瓦亚街（Mohovaya）的布尔萨诺夫家（Bursanovs）去。我打涅瓦河（Neva）边走过，河里封冻着一艘艘覆雪的驳船，轮廓黑沉沉的。这时天已经黑了，街上的人群愈发喧腾。士兵和平民一样，肩上挎着步枪，漫无目的地走来走去。几家酒窖已经被砸开；街上有醉汉；不过反正，人人似乎都醉在革命里了。枪声不时响起，多半是朝天上放的；法院大厦已经葬入火海。众人都觉得，革命已经确立起来了。

===Original===
Curiously enough I had not seen the Bursanovs on my return to Petrograd until that night. They were just the same. Kniaz sat in the corner of the little drawing-room in his usual chair, and it seemed that the revolution had impressed him. But how it had impressed him no one could have divined. Need I say that the three sisters sat in much the same positions, waiting﻿—waiting for developments? Nikolai Vasilievich was very bitter. He had regarded the war almost as a deliberate attempt of providence to complicate his already very complicated domestic situation, and considering that providence had had the satisfaction of achieving its pernicious end, it seemed he could not understand the necessity of a revolution. “Malignity! Malignity!” he muttered, lowering the blinds, as if to show that he, at any rate, would have nothing to do with it.

===Chinese===
说来奇怪，我回到彼得格勒以后，直到那天夜里才见到布尔萨诺夫一家人。他们还是老样子。公爵（Kniaz）坐在小客厅角落他惯常的那把椅子里，革命似乎给他留下了印象。可留下的是什么样的印象，谁也参不透。不消说，三姐妹照旧坐在差不多的老位置上，等着——等着事态发展。尼古拉·瓦西里耶维奇则是怨气冲天。他素来把这场战争几乎看作老天爷处心积虑的一着，专要给他本已千头万绪的家事再添上几分乱；如今老天爷既然心满意足地达到了这个恶毒的目的，他似乎就想不明白：还有什么必要来一场革命。「恶毒！恶毒！」他一面放下遮帘一面咕哝着，仿佛要表示：至少他本人，是决计不肯同它沾边的。

===Original===
“Nothing noble about it at all!” he answered me.

And Fanny Ivanovna, who had been sitting silently for some time, looked as if she were entirely of his opinion on that point.

===Chinese===
「这里头压根儿没有什么高尚的地方！」他这样回答我。

芬妮·伊万诺夫娜（Fanny Ivanovna）已经闷坐了半天，那副神情，仿佛在这一点上完全同意他的看法。

===Original===
And then a horrible groan was heard from the adjoining room. I cast a swift interrogative glance at Nikolai Vasilievich. I had an idea that they had hidden some wretched half-mutilated policeman, the victim of a revolutionary mob. But Nikolai Vasilievich and Fanny Ivanovna looked awkward and ashamed. There was, I noticed, a kind of appeal for sympathy in their eyes.

“That is Fanny Ivanovna’s husband,” said Nikolai Vasilievich apologetically.

===Chinese===
接着，隔壁房间里传出一声可怕的呻吟。我急忙朝尼古拉·瓦西里耶维奇投去一个询问的眼色。我当是他们窝藏了一个叫革命乱民打得半残的倒霉警察。可是尼古拉·瓦西里耶维奇和芬妮·伊万诺夫娜都露出又窘迫又羞惭的神色。我注意到，他们眼睛里有一种恳求同情的意味。

「那是芬妮·伊万诺夫娜的丈夫。」尼古拉·瓦西里耶维奇带着歉意说。

===Original===
I looked incredulous, and he explained. As Fanny Ivanovna was not married to him, she was a German subject, and when war broke out she was to go back to Germany or be interned in Vologda. She refused to go to Germany till Nikolai Vasilievich had provided for her for life, but as the war had further crippled his finances he was not in a position to give her the money; so they married her to Eberheim, an elderly gentleman of German extraction but a Russian subject. As his nominal wife Fanny Ivanovna was a Russian subject and could live in Russia until such time as the improvement in the working of the goldmines made it possible for Nikolai Vasilievich to provide for her for life. Then if he could get a divorce from his wife he would marry Zina.

===Chinese===
我露出不信的神色，他便解释起来。原来芬妮·伊万诺夫娜既然没有同他正式结婚，就还算德国籍；战争一爆发，她要么回德国去，要么就得被拘禁到沃洛格达（Vologda）去。她死活不肯回德国，非要等尼古拉·瓦西里耶维奇给她把终身安顿好不可；偏偏战争又进一步搞垮了他的家底，他实在拿不出这笔钱来；于是他们便把她嫁给了埃伯海姆（Eberheim）——一位上了年纪的德裔老先生，却是俄国籍。作为他的名义妻子，芬妮·伊万诺夫娜便算俄国籍，可以住在俄国，一直住到金矿的经营有了起色、尼古拉·瓦西里耶维奇拿得出钱来供养她终身为止。到那时候，他要是能同妻子离了婚，就娶季娜。

===Original===
“Is he wounded?” I asked, scenting revolutionary blood in the air.

“No﻿—cancer,” said Nikolai Vasilievich. And contrasted with this word painted red, the revolution out of doors seemed pale and trifling.

===Chinese===
「他受了伤吗？」我问，鼻子里嗅出空气中有革命的血腥味。

「不是——是癌症。」尼古拉·瓦西里耶维奇说。同这个涂成红色的字眼一比，门外的革命倒显得苍白而琐屑了。

===Original===
“He is going to have an operation in a day or so.”

“Has he suffered long?” I inquired.

===Chinese===
「他过一两天就要动手术了。」

「他受苦很久了吗？」我问道。

===Original===
“Oh, we took him out of hospital,” explained Fanny Ivanovna. “You may think it odd, but he consented to the marriage on condition that we took him home and looked after him. He said that he would not live long in any case and that money was no earthly use to him in his condition: what he wanted was care and comfort. And now the doctors and operations are costing Nikolai Vasilievich a good bit of money, I can tell you. Really, we are most unfortunate people.﻿ ﻿… And Sonia, too, marrying Baron Wunderhausen, who, as I suspected, is a drag on Nikolai Vasilievich’s resources. Really, he cannot afford it, Andrei Andreiech. The mines﻿—” She waved her hand. Nikolai Vasilievich, with his hands deep in his trouser pockets, stood looking at the window, though the blinds were lowered and there was nothing he could see.

===Chinese===
「噢，是我们把他从医院里接出来的。」芬妮·伊万诺夫娜解释道。「您也许觉得奇怪，可他当初肯应下这门亲事，条件就是要我们把他接回家来照料。他说，反正自己也活不长了，落到他这步田地，钱对他一点儿用处也没有：他要的是有人疼、日子过得安逸。如今请医生、动手术，又要花尼古拉·瓦西里耶维奇好一大笔钱，我可告诉您。说真的，我们是天底下顶倒霉的人家……还有索妮娅（Sonia），又嫁了个温德豪森男爵（Baron Wunderhausen）——不出我所料，这人也是拖累尼古拉·瓦西里耶维奇钱财的一个包袱。说真的，他可负担不起呀，安德烈·安德烈伊奇（Andrei Andreiech）。那些矿哪——」她把手一挥。尼古拉·瓦西里耶维奇两手深深插在裤袋里，站在那儿望着窗户出神，虽然遮帘早已放下，其实什么也望不见。

===Original===
“The wedding ceremony,” she went on, “was painful. I barely stood it myself. The priest at first refused to marry us. Nikolai Vasilievich had to lead him aside and bribe him. Eberheim’s condition was so bad﻿ ﻿… critical. It was wicked.﻿ ﻿… Yet he has a way of lasting. He has lasted now for over two years. One wants to be human to him, but really, Andrei Andreiech, look at us, look at us﻿ ﻿… us.﻿ ﻿… And now the revolution. Who *wants* the revolution?” She put her chin on her hand and turned her face away. There was silence.

===Chinese===
「那场婚礼，」她接着说，「真是受罪。我自己都差一点儿撑不住。神甫起先死活不肯给我们证婚。尼古拉·瓦西里耶维奇只得把他拉到一边，塞了贿赂才罢休。埃伯海姆当时的病情重得很……都到危殆的份上了。真是作孽……可他偏偏有本事这么熬着。这一熬，已经熬了两年多。谁都想待他厚道些，可是说真的，安德烈·安德烈伊奇，您瞧瞧我们，瞧瞧我们……瞧瞧我们……如今又来了革命。谁*要*这个革命呀？」她把下巴支在手背上，把脸扭了过去。屋里一片静默。

===Original===
Then, suddenly, without reason or provocation, she turned on the old Kniaz, sitting neatly in his usual armchair, imperturbable like a butler:

“Kniaz! Don’t sit there like that, like.﻿ ﻿… Oh, God, you’ve been sitting like that in that chair for thirteen years.﻿ ﻿… Say something! Say something!”

===Chinese===
随后，冷不防地，无缘无故，也没谁招惹她，她便冲老公爵发作起来——他端端正正坐在自己那把惯常的扶手椅里，不动声色，活像一位老管家：

「公爵！别那么坐在那儿，那么……唉，老天爷，您就这么在那把椅子里坐了十三年了……说句什么吧！说句什么吧！」

===Original===
“What can I say?” he smiled faintly.

“What can you say!” she echoed; and again there was silence.

“Hasn’t he got any relations?” I asked.

===Chinese===
「我能说什么呢？」他淡淡一笑。

「你能说出什么来！」她学着他的话音应道；接着又是一片静默。

「他就没有一点亲眷吗？」我问。

===Original===
She shook her head.

“No money?”

“Penniless.”

===Chinese===
她摇了摇头。

「也没有钱？」

「身无分文。」

===Original===
“Is he﻿ ﻿… good?”

“Yes, but﻿ ﻿… exacting.”

“Oh, poor fellow, he can’t help it,” said Nikolai Vasilievich.

===Chinese===
「他……人品还好吗？」

「好是好，可是……难伺候。」

「唉，可怜的人，他自己也做不得主哇。」尼古拉·瓦西里耶维奇说。

===Original===
“Poor fellow,” said Fanny Ivanovna.

“Poor fellow,” I echoed.

For a moment we sat in silence. We waited for Eberheim to groan again; but he too was silent; and we could just hear the measured ticking of the great oak-panelled clock in the corner and the subdued tumult of the streets below.

===Chinese===
「可怜的人哪。」芬妮·伊万诺夫娜说。

「可怜的人哪。」我也跟着说。

有那么一会儿，我们默默地坐着。我们都等着埃伯海姆再呻吟一声；可他也一声不响；只听得见墙角那座橡木嵌板大钟匀整的滴答声，和楼下街上隐隐约约的喧腾。

===Original===
“And where is Magda Nikolaevna?” I asked.

“She is with Cecedek.”

“One burden less, what, Nikolai Vasilievich?”

===Chinese===
「那么玛格达·尼古拉耶夫娜（Magda Nikolaevna）在哪儿呢？」我问。

「她跟塞塞德克（Cecedek）在一块儿。」

「这下少了一副担子，是不是，尼古拉·瓦西里耶维奇？」

===Original===
Nikolai Vasilievich sighed.

“You would hardly say so,” said Fanny Ivanovna, “for Nikolai Vasilievich still has to keep his wife.”

“But what of Cecedek?”

===Chinese===
尼古拉·瓦西里耶维奇叹了口气。

「话可不能这么说，」芬妮·伊万诺夫娜说，「因为尼古拉·瓦西里耶维奇还得养活他的太太呢。」

「那塞塞德克本人怎么样呢？」

===Original===
“I am very sorry for him,” said Fanny Ivanovna.

And I learnt that at the outbreak of hostilities the Russian authorities had found it necessary to confiscate the whole of Cecedek’s property. They were then going to intern him, but he succeeded in proving to them that he was now a Czech; and so they set him free. But the property which they had taken from him as an Austrian they did not return to him as a Czech. He had been in correspondence with the authorities on this subject ever since July, 1914, and on his ultimate success in getting some of it refunded his marriage with Magda Nikolaevna must henceforth depend. Whether the revolution would assist him in his ambitious expectations, or would delay them further, it was hard to prophesy. Nikolai Vasilievich helped him as much as he was able in his present circumstances. In the meantime Magda Nikolaevna had suspended her application for a divorce and was still on Nikolai Vasilievich’s books for payment. But Cecedek’s attitude had not changed. He now rather liked to emphasize the Slavic side to their union, had in the last three years developed a Czech intonation in his Russian speech, professed an undue regard for his “Brother-Slavs,” pronounced his own name “Chechedek,” and in signing put those funny little accents on the *C*’s.

===Chinese===
「我很替他难过。」芬妮·伊万诺夫娜说。

我这才知道：交战一开，俄国当局认为有必要把塞塞德克的财产悉数没收。他们原打算把他本人也拘禁起来，但他成功地向当局证明了如今自己已是捷克人；于是他们放了他。可是，当初以奥地利人的身份从他手里拿走的财产，却没有以捷克人的身份还给他。为这件事，他从一九一四年七月起就同当局书信往来，至今不断；他日后能不能讨回其中一部分，他和玛格达·尼古拉耶夫娜的婚事，往后便全悬在这上头了。革命究竟会成全他那野心勃勃的指望呢，还是把它更加拖延下去，这就难乎预言了。尼古拉·瓦西里耶维奇在眼下这种境况里，能帮他的也都帮了。这期间，玛格达·尼古拉耶夫娜撤回了离婚申请，开销照旧记在尼古拉·瓦西里耶维奇的账上。可塞塞德克的态度丝毫未变。他如今倒颇喜欢强调这桩结合里斯拉夫的一面，近三年来又在自己的俄语里养成了一副捷克腔，对「斯拉夫兄弟」表露出一种过分的敬意，把自己的名字念成「切切德克」（Chechedek），签名时还要在 *C* 字母上添上那些滑稽的小变音符号。

===Original===
I left them very early next morning; in the excitement of the day there had been much work left overnight unaccomplished. It was about six o’clock when I crossed the Field of Mars. Soldiers in odd groups strolled along in the snow, now and then firing off a rifle in the air, just for the fun of the thing; and the capital wore that appearance of a banqueting-hall in the shrewd light of the morning after a particularly heavy feast. Fretful clouds moved swiftly across the winter sky. The morning promised a fine day.

===Chinese===
第二天一早我便离开了他们；白天那阵兴奋里，好些活计过了夜还没有做完。约莫六点钟光景，我穿过了战神广场（Field of Mars）。士兵们三三两两，在雪地里信步溜达，时不时朝天放上一枪，纯粹为了好玩；在清晨凛冽犀利的天光里，京城显出一副大宴之后翌晨宴厅的景象——那场酒宴实在是太酣了。焦躁的云朵迅疾掠过冬日的天空。照这天早晨看来，倒像会是个好天。

### III / 三

===Original===

The revolution dragged on through the winter and “deepened” as the months advanced. The forerunners of confusion became visible: food and commodities were being procured in an irregular manner. All were waiting.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

革命拖拖拉拉地挨过了那个冬天，还随着月份的推移而不断「深化」。混乱的先兆已然显露：食物和日用品，都得靠非正规的门路去弄了。人人都在等待……

===Original===

Pictures of them recur continually to my mind, as I write. I can see Fanny Ivanovna, and particularly I can see the three sisters, always sitting in the same positions, perched on sundry chairs and sofas, Fanny Ivanovna engaged in silent contemplation over needlework, and Kniaz sitting in his usual chair, reading, or more often sitting idly, thinking into space. The seasons would be changing rapidly from one to the other﻿—but their position never! Rain would drum against the windowpane, snow would be falling on the street below; then the ice on the Neva would begin to break and slowly move toward the Bay; and again one would feel the onset of spring, the unfolding of white nights.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

如今我一提笔，他们的形象便不断浮上心头。我看得见芬妮·伊万诺夫娜（Fanny Ivanovna），尤其看得见那三姐妹，永远坐在同样的位置上，栖在形形色色的椅子沙发之间——芬妮·伊万诺夫娜对着针线活儿默默出神，公爵（Kniaz）坐在他惯常的椅子上，读读书，或者多半只是闲坐着，对着虚空想心事。季节飞快地一个接一个更替——可他们坐的位置从来不换！雨点会敲打窗玻璃，街上会落雪；随后涅瓦河（Neva）上的冰开始崩裂，缓缓向芬兰湾漂去；接着又让人觉出春意将至，白夜徐徐铺展……

===Original===

“How tiring this is, Andrei Andreiech,” Fanny Ivanovna complained. “To be always waiting to begin to *live*. When is that upward movement in happiness, that splendid life that we are always waiting for, to begin at last? Somehow you wait for the spring. But spring has come﻿ ﻿… *alone*, and only emphasizes our misery, by the contrast.﻿ ﻿… Spring makes me mad. I begin to want impossible things.﻿ ﻿…”

“You are an active woman, Fanny Ivanovna,” said I. “You ought not to sit still. It’s bad for you. You ought to run about.”

===Chinese===

「这多叫人疲倦呀，安德烈·安德烈伊奇，」芬妮·伊万诺夫娜抱怨道。「总是等着去开始*生活*。那幸福的上行，那我们一直等着的美好生活，究竟几时才能开始？不知怎么，人总要盼着春天。可春天来了……*独自*来了，只凭这一对照，便把我们的苦况衬得愈发分明……春天叫我发疯。我开始想要些不可能的东西了……」

「您是个闲不住的人，芬妮·伊万诺夫娜，」我说。「不该老这么坐着不动。这对您不好。您该多四处跑跑。」

===Original===

“But﻿ ﻿… I’ve got to wait.”

“I suppose waiting is sitting still. It *is*, in a sense.﻿ ﻿…”

“It isn’t that. But what am I going to run about for? I go out shopping. But that doesn’t *advance* things, you understand. Besides, I simply dread asking Nikolai Vasilievich for money.”

===Chinese===

「可是……我总得等呀。」

「依我看，等待就是坐着不动。从某种意义上讲，也*确是*如此……」

「不是那么回事。可我干吗要四处跑呢？我出去买东西呀。可那*推动*不了事情，您明白的。再说，跟尼古拉·瓦西里耶维奇开口要钱，我简直怕得要死。」

===Original===

“He hasn’t got any?”

“He has. He’s always borrowing﻿—*crescendo, forte, fortissimo!* But where will it end? When? Borrowing money is all right if you can do it. But it’s not, as it were, an income; it’s not﻿—how shall I put it?﻿—an end in itself, is it? There’s got to be something, somewhere, sometime. Those goldmines have got to justify themselves. Our plans, our movements, everything depends on them. That’s why it’s so annoying. They’ve got to pay, and I am confident that they will pay. But *when*?﻿ ﻿…”

===Chinese===

「他手头没钱？」

「有。他老是去借——*crescendo，forte，fortissimo*（渐强，强，极强）！可这要借到哪儿才算完？几时算完？借钱这事，只要借得着，本也无可厚非。可这算不上，譬如说，一笔进项；也算不上——该怎么讲呢？——本身就是目的的东西，对不对？总得有点什么才行，在某个地方，在某个时候。那些金矿总得证明自己物有所值。我们的计划、我们的行动，样样都指靠着它们。所以才这么恼人。它们非得出钱不可，我也确信它们会出钱。可是*什么时候*？……」

===Original===

She rose abruptly, as was her wont, her black silk skirt rustling as she swept out.

It was “Papa this” and “Mamma that” and “Fanny Ivanovna the other thing.”

===Chinese===

她霍地站起身来——这是她的老习惯——黑绸裙子窸窣一响，一阵风似的走了出去。

谈来谈去，不外是「爸爸这一桩」「妈妈那一桩」「芬妮·伊万诺夫娜另外一桩」。

===Original===

“Won’t you stop sighing?” I suggested.

“It’s all very well for you,” protested the three sisters simultaneously. “But do you think it’s very nice for us?”

“What do you want, anyhow?”

They did not answer; they looked at the window, brooding.

===Chinese===

「你们就不能不叹气了吗？」我提议道。

「你说得倒轻巧，」三姐妹齐声抗议道。「可你想想，我们这样好受吗？」

「那你们究竟想要什么？」

她们不答话；她们望着窗子，郁郁地想心事。

===Original===

I said in a jovial tone of voice:

“Well, I tried to help you. But you won’t be helped.”

“Helped us indeed!” they cried out simultaneously. The three sisters had a way of speaking simultaneously and almost word for word in matters of domestic politics. They were a party in themselves, stubbornly opposed to all the other camps of Nikolai Vasilievich’s family.

===Chinese===

我用快活的声调说：

「好吧，我是想帮你们的。可你们不肯受人帮。」

「帮我们，亏你说得出口！」她们齐声喊道。三姐妹有个本事：凡属家庭内政的事，总是异口同声，而且几乎一字不差。她们自成一边，顽强地同尼古拉·瓦西里耶维奇家的其他各营垒作对。

===Original===

The night before, I had taken them to Kusivitski’s concert. People had been staring enviously at me, as if to ask: “Who are those three pretty kittens?” I felt absurdly like a proud papa. The music was excruciating. During the piano solo I clung to my chair: I could scarcely sit still. “Scriabin,” I burst out as the music stopped, “is a persistent knocking at the door﻿—but the door doesn’t open. Still, as we might know in any case that there *is* nothing behind the door, that doesn’t greatly matter, does it? It’s the knocking that is a human necessity. And what a desperate knocking it is!”

===Chinese===

前一天晚上，我带她们去听了库谢维茨基（Kusivitski）的音乐会。人们眼红地盯着我瞧，仿佛在问：「这三只漂亮的小猫儿是谁家的？」我荒唐地自觉像个骄傲的老爸爸。那音乐难听得钻心。钢琴独奏时，我死死扒住椅子：简直坐不住。琴声一停，我脱口而出：「斯克里亚宾（Scriabin）就是一阵执拗的敲门声——可门就是不开。不过，反正我们横竖知道门后头*什么也没有*，那也就无关紧要了，对不对？敲门才是人的一种必需。而且这敲门敲得多么绝望啊！」

===Original===

Nina looked at me with that trick she had of assuming innocence and said: “Which door?”

And it flashed across my mind that, whereas Sonia played the piano with an agreeable touch of feeling, Nina’s hammering was shrill and disagreeable, while, musically, Vera was still an unknown quantity.

===Chinese===

尼娜（Nina）带着她那套佯装无辜的老把戏望着我，说：「哪扇门？」

我脑子里忽然闪过一个念头：索妮娅（Sonia）弹钢琴，指下自有一缕动人的情致；尼娜的敲敲打打却尖利刺耳；而薇拉（Vera）在音乐上，还是个未知数。

===Original===

But the pianist had resumed.

“What is this?” Nina asked.

“A foxtrot,” I replied, very superior.

===Chinese===

可钢琴家又弹起来了。

「这是什么？」尼娜问。

「一支狐步舞曲，」我答道，一副高人一等的神气。

===Original===

I sat on the small seat facing the three sisters, as Professor Metchnikoff trotted homeward through the sombre streets. The night was warm and humid. By the street lamps I could see their faces. When she was silent Nina looked so wise. Perhaps she seemed wiser than she actually was. All this﻿—the war, the revolution﻿—she had overlooked: and it did not exist. Scriabin﻿—she had overlooked him. And he did not exist. But she was there, watchful.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

我坐在三姐妹对面的小座位上，梅契尼科夫教授（Metchnikoff）正一路小跑，穿过阴沉沉的街道回家去。夜温暖而潮湿。借着街灯，我看得清她们的脸。尼娜沉默的时候，显得那么聪明。也许她显得比实际上还要聪明。这一切——战争啦、革命啦——她一概视而不见：于是它们便不存在。斯克里亚宾——她把他视而不见了。于是他也不存在。可她在那儿，警醒地守望着……

===Original===

The day after was like the day before. They sat there listless﻿—Fanny Ivanovna, Kniaz and the three sisters. The three sisters always sat in some extraordinary positions, on the backs of sofas and easy chairs, and Fanny Ivanovna and Kniaz sat in very ordinary positions. Nikolai Vasilievich alone was always absent; and I think there was a sort of feeling running through us all that he at least was busy, *doing* something. But in more sceptical moods I know I was inclined to question dubiously whether he too was *getting* anywhere for all the semblance of activity that his mysterious absence involved. I remember the silhouette of Nina’s profile at the window. I can feel the tension of the silence that hung over the room, the suspense of waiting﻿—of indefinite waiting for indefinite things. In the hush that had crept upon us I could fancy I could sense acutely the disturbing presence of the things my eye could not behold: the gilded domes radiant in the fading sunlight, the many bridges thrown across the widespread stream; and in the stillness I was made to feel as if by instinct the throbbing pulse of Petrograd. The leaden waves splashed gently against the granite banks; and the air was full of that yearning melancholy call of life that yet reminds one﻿—God knows why﻿—of the imminence of death; and in the sky there was the promise of a white night.

===Chinese===

第二天同前一天一个样。他们无精打采地坐在那儿——芬妮·伊万诺夫娜、公爵，还有三姐妹。三姐妹总坐出些奇特的姿势，栖在沙发和安乐椅的靠背上；芬妮·伊万诺夫娜和公爵的坐相则平平无奇。唯独尼古拉·瓦西里耶维奇总是不在；而且我想，我们大家心里都隐隐有个感觉：至少他是在忙，是在*做*事。可在疑心更重的时刻，我知道我不免犯嘀咕：他那神神秘秘的缺席纵然裹着一层忙碌的外衣，他自己究竟是不是也在*奔*出个什么名堂。我记得尼娜倚在窗前的侧影。我至今感觉得到当时笼罩全屋的寂静的张力，等待的悬心——为说不清的东西作没完没了的等待。在悄然袭上我们的静默里，我仿佛能敏锐地觉出肉眼看不见的事物那扰人的存在：在渐渐黯淡的日光里熠熠生辉的镀金穹顶，横跨开阔河面的重重桥梁；在寂静中，我仿佛出于本能，感到了彼得格勒那怦然搏动的脉搏。铅灰色的波浪轻轻拍击着花岗岩的堤岸；空气里满是生命那含着渴慕的哀愁呼唤，而这呼唤——天知道为什么——又叫人想起死亡的迫近；天空中，已经透出一个白夜的许诺。

### IV / 四

===Original===
Petrograd looked thoroughly nasty on that cold November morning. There was the drizzling snow, and it was still dark as I walked home with Uncle Kostia. We had been at the Finland Station to see off two of Uncle Kostia’s nieces who were going abroad. It was the morning of the Bolshevik Revolution, and Uncle Kostia looked pessimistic. “Do you remember all those student-revolutionaries, the heroes of our young intelligentsia, who had been persecuted by the old regime? Well,”﻿—he pointed from the bridge that we were traversing to the Bolshevik craft that had arrived from Kronstadt overnight﻿—“*this* is more than they bargained for. More than they bargained for.”

We walked on.
===Chinese===
那个寒冷的十一月清晨，彼得格勒（Petrograd）看上去一副彻头彻尾的阴恶相。细雪蒙蒙地下着，我陪科斯佳叔叔（Uncle Kostia）往家走的路上，天色还是黑的。我们刚在芬兰车站（Finland Station）给科斯佳叔叔两位出国去的侄女送了行。这天正是布尔什维克（Bolsheviks）革命的早晨，科斯佳叔叔满面悲观。「你还记得当年那些学生革命家，我们青年知识界的那些英雄，受旧政权迫害的那批人吗？瞧，」——他站在我们正走过的大桥上，指着夜里从喀琅施塔得（Kronstadt）驶来的布尔什维克船只——「*这*可比他们当初讨的价码多得多。多得多啊。」

我们继续朝前走。

===Original===
“They are malcontents again﻿—but on the other side! Truth is fond of playing practical jokes of this sort. My God! how elusive it is. It is wonderful how beneath our hastily made-up truths, the truths of usage and convenience, there runs independently, often contrariwise, a wider, bigger truth. Can’t you feel it? The pseudo-reason of unreason. The lack of reasonable evidence in reason. Issues, motives being muddled up. This ethical confusion, and the blind habitual resort to bloodshed as a means of straightening it out. More confusion. Honour is involved. Bloodshed as a solution. More honour involved in the solution. More bloodshed. That idiotic plea that each generation should sacrifice itself for the so-called benefit of the next! It never seems to end.﻿ ﻿… Oh, how the pendulum swings! Wider and wider, and we are shedding blood generation after generation. For *what*? For *whom*?﻿ ﻿… For future generations! My God, what fools we are! Fools shedding blood for the sake of future fools, who will do as much!”

“But what are you to do? *What?*” I persisted.

Uncle Kostia was evasive. “You see,” he said at last, “subtleties of the mind, if pursued to their logical conclusions, become crudities. Let us cease our conversation at this point.”
===Chinese===
「他们又是一班愤懑不平之徒了——只不过这回站到了另一边！真理就爱开这一类恶作剧的玩笑。天哪，它多难捉摸。妙就妙在，在我们匆匆编就的那些真理——因袭惯用、图个方便的真理——底下，有一条更宽、更大的真理在独自奔流，还常常反着道流。你感觉不出来吗？非理性之中的伪理性。理性之中合理证据的匮乏。问题也好，动机也罢，搅作一团。再加上这伦理上的混沌，以及盲目成习地诉诸流血，拿流血当捋直乱麻的手段。于是更乱了。荣誉牵涉了进来。流血成了解决办法。解决办法里又牵进更多荣誉。更多流血。还有那套蠢不可及的论调，说每一代人都该为下一代的所谓利益牺牲掉自己！这架势仿佛永无了局……唉，这钟摆摆得多厉害！越摆越宽，我们一代接一代地流着血。为了*什么*？为了*谁*？……为了后代！天哪，我们是何等的傻瓜！傻瓜为着将来的傻瓜流血，而那些傻瓜还会照样流个够！」

「可您又有什么办法呢？*什么*办法？」我追问不休。

科斯佳叔叔闪避着。「您瞧，」他末了说道，「头脑里精微的东西，倘若推究到逻辑的尽头，就会变成粗陋的货色。我们的谈话，就到此为止吧。」

===Original===
Barricades appeared in the streets. Bridges were being suspended. Lorries of joyriding proletarians became familiarly conspicuous, as I walked on towards the Bursanovs.

I found the household in a state of wild excitement. However, the event had no connection with the Revolution. In fact, with continual domestic revolutions in their own home, the much ado about the political revolution appeared, particularly to the three sisters, a foolish affectation.

I learnt that Nikolai Vasilievich had just discovered that his bookkeeper Stanitski, at the instigation of his house-agent, had these last five years been falsifying the books and robbing him wholesale. When the discovery was made the house-agent had vanished into the darkness whence he had emerged. But as I entered I was very nearly knocked down by Nikolai Vasilievich dashing after Stanitski, the bookkeeper, as he was flying down the stairs. He caught him by the tail of his overcoat and dragged him back into his study. He had him standing, stiff and awkward and ashamed, before his desk, while he himself reclined in his armchair.
===Chinese===
街上出现了街垒。一座座桥梁被吊了起来。满载着兜风取乐的无产者的卡车，已是司空见惯的显眼景致——我一路朝布尔萨诺夫家（Bursanovs）走去。

我发现这一家子正处在一片狂乱的兴奋之中。然而，这桩大事与革命毫不相干。事实上，他们自己家里的「家庭革命」本就一场接一场，对这场政治革命的小题大做，在他们看来——尤其在三姊妹看来——简直是一种愚蠢的装腔作势。

我这才得知，尼古拉·瓦西里耶维奇（Nikolai Vasilievich）刚刚发现：他的账房先生斯坦尼茨基（Stanitski）受人唆使——唆使的正是他的房产经纪人——这五年来一直在账上作假，大宗大宗地偷他的钱。事情一败露，那房产经纪人便缩回了他冒出来的那片黑暗里，无影无踪。而我进门的时候，险些被尼古拉·瓦西里耶维奇撞个正着——他正追赶账房先生斯坦尼茨基，那人飞也似的往楼下逃。他一把揪住他大衣的下摆，把他拖回书房，叫他直挺挺、僵生生、满面羞惭地立在书桌前，自己则往扶手椅里一靠。

===Original===
Nikolai Vasilievich did not shout, as Stanitski, who knew his master intimately, had, no doubt, expected him to do. He spoke quietly and even sadly; and it was the sadness of his speech that penetrated Stanitski’s Slavic nature to the heart. “How could you have cheated me like that, Ivan Sergeiech﻿—me who have trusted you?”

And Stanitski became emotional. “Nikolai Vasilievich!” he exclaimed with his hands joined together and the whites of his eyes turned heavenward. “Nikolai Vasilievich! God in Heaven knows I have not been helping myself to your money, as you seem to think, recklessly. But since I took a little﻿—and I have a wife, children, dependents﻿—I had to do what the house-agent told me. I was in his hands, at the mercy of a blackguard and a robber. Nikolai Vasilievich: I often felt I wanted to warn you of this rascal. But I was in his hands﻿ ﻿… since I took myself. But I took in measure, Nikolai Vasilievich, conscientiously, with my eyes on God.﻿ ﻿…”

The old man sobbed bitter tears. He felt that fate had dealt him a cruel blow, unjustifiably cruel, in return for his moderation.
===Chinese===
尼古拉·瓦西里耶维奇并没有大吼大叫——斯坦尼茨基深知自己东家的脾性，毫无疑问，他原本等着的就是一顿吼。他说话声音平静，甚至还带着伤感；而正是话里这份伤感，直刺入斯坦尼茨基那斯拉夫天性的心底。「你怎么能这样骗我，伊万·谢尔盖伊奇（Ivan Sergeiech）——骗我这个信得过你的人？」

于是斯坦尼茨基动了感情。「尼古拉·瓦西里耶维奇！」他双手合十，两眼翻起眼白望着天，大声喊道。「尼古拉·瓦西里耶维奇！皇天上帝知道，我可没有像您想的那样，不管不顾地大把揩您的钱。不过，既然我拿了一点儿——我有老婆，有孩子，还有一窝要我养活的人——我就只能照房产经纪人的吩咐去做。我落在他手里，听凭一个恶棍、一个强盗的发落。尼古拉·瓦西里耶维奇，我常常觉得该提醒您防着这个歹徒。可我落在他手里呀……谁叫我自己先伸手拿了。不过我拿得有分寸，尼古拉·瓦西里耶维奇，我凭良心拿，眼睛望着上帝拿……」

老人淌下了辛酸的眼泪，抽咽不已。他觉得，命运回敬他的是一记残忍的打击，残忍得不近情理——要知道，他行事可是那样有节制。

===Original===
What could be done to him? Baron Wunderhausen, who now, as Sonia’s husband, lived with the family, suggested handing the man over to the Bolshevik militia. But Nikolai Vasilievich only waved his hand. I think it was the family aspect of the old man’s position that penetrated Nikolai Vasilievich to the heart. He sat there at his desk, brooding darkly, while Stanitski, gently, like a cat, felt his way out of the flat.

Fanny Ivanovna sighed conspicuously.

“An optimistic gentleman﻿—Stanitski,” I remarked. “What a belief in the kindliness of things! What a claim on the favour of Providence!”
===Chinese===
能把他怎么样呢？温德豪森男爵（Baron Wunderhausen）——他如今身为索妮娅（Sonia）的丈夫，同这一家人住在一起——提议把此人扭送布尔什维克民兵。可尼古拉·瓦西里耶维奇只是摆了摆手。我想，刺入尼古拉·瓦西里耶维奇心底的，正是这位老人处境中家室的那一面。他坐在书桌前，阴郁地出着神；而斯坦尼茨基则轻轻地、像猫一样，摸索着溜出了这套公寓。

芬妮·伊万诺夫娜（Fanny Ivanovna）长叹了一声，叹得响亮而分明。

「真是个乐天派的绅士——这位斯坦尼茨基，」我评论道。「对世道的仁慈，是何等的信念！对上天的恩宠，是何等的索取！」

===Original===
“And, as it happens, he is not far wrong in his calculations,” said the Baron with a bitterness which showed that he, as son-in-law, was dissatisfied with the management of the family’s finances. “I call this state of things disgraceful.”

“God have mercy upon us!” whispered Fanny Ivanovna, almost ironically.
===Chinese===
「而且，巧就巧在，他这笔账算得也不算离谱，」男爵说，话里带着一股酸苦，显出他这个做女婿的，对这家钱财的打理心怀不满。「依我看，这局面简直不成体统。」

「上帝可怜可怜我们吧！」芬妮·伊万诺夫娜低声说，语气近乎讥诮。

===Original===
“An optimist,” I digressed aloud, “is a fool, since he can’t see what awaits him﻿—disillusionment. But he is wise without knowing it, since, however bad the present, he remains an optimist as to the future, and so his present seems never quite so bad to him as it really is.”

“Say it again,” breathed Nina.

“A fool,” said Nikolai Vasilievich, “is an optimist. He is optimistic about himself, optimistic about his folly. *I*’m an optimist!”
===Chinese===
「乐天派呢，」我出声地把话岔开，「是个傻瓜，因为他看不见等着他的是什么——幻灭。可他同时又是个不自知的智者，因为无论眼下多么糟，他对未来始终是个乐天派，于是他的当下，在他看来就永远不像实际上那么糟。」

「再讲一遍。」尼娜（Nina）轻声吐出一句。

「傻瓜呢，」尼古拉·瓦西里耶维奇说，「就是个乐天派。他对自个儿乐观，对自个儿的荒唐也乐观。*我*就是个乐天派！」

===Original===
He stood up, his hands in his trouser pockets, and gazed at the window. Twilight was falling swiftly. Nina, perched up on the sofa, sat silent, her head bent.

“What’s the good of being miserable?” I said to her.
===Chinese===
他站起身来，双手插在裤袋里，凝望着窗子。暮色正急速地降下来。尼娜高高蜷坐在沙发上，默默无语，垂着头。

「愁眉苦脸的，有什么用呢？」我对她说。

===Original===
“As though I deliberately chose to be miserable!” To console herself, she took an apple.

“Optimists that we are!” sighed Fanny Ivanovna.

“Warranting considerable pessimism,” supplied the Baron.
===Chinese===
「倒像是我存心拣了愁苦来受似的！」她拿起一只苹果，聊以自慰。

「我们可真是些乐天派！」芬妮·伊万诺夫娜叹道。

「这足以为不小的悲观作保了。」男爵接口道。

===Original===
“It is easier to hope,” said Nikolai Vasilievich, “and be disappointed, it is easier to hope knowing that one will be disappointed, than not hope at all.”

“Why don’t the writers﻿—the novelists﻿—why don’t they write about this, this real life,” said Fanny Ivanovna, “this real drama of life, rather than their neat, reasoned, reasonable and﻿—oh! so unconvincing novels?”

“This philosophizing won’t help us,” jeered Nikolai Vasilievich mildly. “We ought to *do* things. I want to *do* things. This moment I am teeming over with energy. I could do and settle things today, square up our affairs, and start life afresh.﻿ ﻿… But.﻿ ﻿…”
===Chinese===
「怀着希望，然后失望，」尼古拉·瓦西里耶维奇说，「明知会失望仍怀着希望——这总还是比较容易的，比压根儿不抱希望容易。」

「那些作家——写小说的——为什么不写写这个，写写这种真实的生活，」芬妮·伊万诺夫娜说，「写写生活里真正的戏剧，倒偏要去写他们那些干净利落、条分缕析、合情合理——哎！却又那么不能服人的小说呢？」

「这套空谈的哲学帮不了我们什么忙，」尼古拉·瓦西里耶维奇温和地讥诮道。「我们应当*做*点事情。我就想*做*点事情。此刻我浑身是劲，满得直往外溢。我今天就能把各样事情办妥、了结，把家底理顺，重新开始生活……可是。……」

===Original===
The Baron looked at him. “Well?”

“But﻿—” A gesture at the window indicated the obstacle. “What can I do with *this*? What can anybody do? All is tumbling, going to ruin. In a month or so all business will stop, works will close down. The rouble will be valueless. There will be *nothing*.﻿ ﻿…”
===Chinese===
男爵望着他。「嗯？」

「可是——」朝窗子的一挥手，指明了障碍所在。「对*这个*我能有什么办法？谁又能有什么办法？一切都在崩塌，都在走向毁灭。再过个把月，一切买卖都要停摆，工厂都要关门。卢布将一文不值。到那时就*什么*都没有了……」

===Original===
“Now don’t lose courage, Nikolai,” said Fanny Ivanovna hopefully. “We shall pull through; somehow we shall; and then on the other side of the grave we shall be safe.”

“Her most optimistic moment in life!” jeered Nikolai Vasilievich.
===Chinese===
「可别泄气，尼古拉，」芬妮·伊万诺夫娜满怀希望地说。「我们会熬过去的；总有法子熬过去；等熬到坟墓的那一头，我们就平安无事了。」

「她这辈子最乐观的时刻！」尼古拉·瓦西里耶维奇讥诮道。

===Original===
“It’s a surprising thing what the human soul will stand, Andrei Andreiech,” she said. “I can venture only this explanation; it is habit. You see, the cup is ever filled to the brim, but﻿—lo! the miracle! the cup expands. No trouble. None!﻿ ﻿… And here we are.”

“Life gets you,” came from the window; “sooner or later it gets you all the same.”
===Chinese===
「人的灵魂受得住些什么，真是桩惊人的事，安德烈·安德烈伊奇，」她说。「我只敢提出这一种解释：习惯。您瞧，杯子永远斟得齐了边，可是——瞧啊！奇迹来了！杯子自己涨大了。不碍事。一点儿不碍事！……于是我们好端端还在这儿。」

「生活总要逮住你，」窗边传来一句，「早也罢，晚也罢，横竖总要逮住你。」

===Original===
“I don’t know what it’s for, why, or who wants it. It seems so unnecessary, useless, even silly. And yet I cannot think that it’s all in vain. There must be﻿ ﻿… perhaps a larger pattern somewhere in which all these futilities, these shifting incongruities are somehow reconciled. But shall we know? Shall we ever know the reason?”

“Philosophy!” jeered Nikolai Vasilievich mildly.
===Chinese===
「我不知道这一切为的什么，为什么，又是谁要它这样。它显得那么多余、无用，甚至愚蠢。然而我又不能认为这一切全是枉然。必定有……或许在什么地方，有一个更大的图样，所有这些徒劳、这些变幻不定的扞格，总会以某种方式在其中归于调和。可我们会知道吗？我们到底会不会知道那个缘由？」

「又是哲学！」尼古拉·瓦西里耶维奇温和地讥诮道。

===Original===
“Perhaps,” I said, “when we awaken on the other side of death and ask to be told the reason, they will shrug their shoulders and will say, ‘We don’t know. It is beyond us. Do *you* not know?’﻿ ﻿… And we shall never know. *Never.﻿ ﻿…*”

“How awfully funnily your mouth moves when you speak,” said Nina, who had been listening to me attentively.

“Frightfully!”
===Chinese===
「也许，」我说，「等我们在死亡的那一头醒来，请求人家把缘由讲给我们听，他们会耸耸肩，说：『我们不知道。这不是我们所能知道的。*你们*自己不知道吗？』……而我们将永远不知道。*永远。……*」

「你说话的时候，嘴巴动得滑稽极了，」尼娜说；她方才一直在专心听我说话。

「滑稽得吓人！」

===Original===
“There is no proof,” said Baron Wunderhausen, “that death *is* the end. But there is no proof, as yet, that death is *not* the end.”

“So there is no proof of anything?” asked Nikolai Vasilievich.

“No.”
===Chinese===
「并没有证据，」温德豪森男爵说，「能证明死亡*就是*终点。但也还没有证据，能证明死亡*不是*终点。」

「这么说来，就什么事都没有证据了？」尼古拉·瓦西里耶维奇问。

「没有。」

===Original===
“Thank you,” said Fanny Ivanovna.

The Baron bowed.
===Chinese===
「谢谢你。」芬妮·伊万诺夫娜说。

男爵鞠了一躬。

===Original===
Then Nikolai Vasilievich passed into the hall and put his coat on. As it was time for me to go, we went out together. I remember there was something hopeless about that night, a sense of dread about the political and economic chaos, that seemed to harmonize with Nikolai Vasilievich’s state of mind. I think it may be that he found a kind of ghastly pleasure in the thought that if he was miserable, if destitution stared him in the face, the whole world also seemed to be tumbling about him into decay and ruin. As we crossed the Palace Square we were challenged by a soldier who had emerged from behind a pile of firewood dumped before the Winter Palace. He stepped forward with fixed bayonet and demanded money, while pointing his bayonet at my breast; he held his finger on the trigger. He was considerably drunk. Neither of us happened to have any money. “Got any cigarettes, Comrades?” he asked.
===Chinese===
随后尼古拉·瓦西里耶维奇走进门厅，穿上了大衣。我也到了该告辞的时候，我们便一同出门。我记得那天晚上有一种万念俱灰的意味，一种对政治与经济乱局的忧惧，而这忧惧似乎正与尼古拉·瓦西里耶维奇的心境相谐。我想，他也许从这样一个念头里尝到了一种阴惨的快意：倘若他困顿不堪，倘若穷厄已瞪眼盯着他，那么整个世界似乎也正在他周围坍塌，一同朽烂、毁灭。我们穿过宫殿广场（Palace Square）时，一个士兵喝住了我们；他从冬宫（Winter Palace）前卸下的一垛柴火后面闪出身来，端着上了刺刀的枪逼上前来要钱，刺刀直指着我的胸口；他的指头扣在扳机上。他已经醉得相当可以。我们俩碰巧谁都没带钱。「有烟吗，同志？」他问。

===Original===
Neither of us had cigarettes.

“And I,” explained the drunken soldier, “go about, you know, letting the guts out of the bourgouys.”

“That’s right, Comrade,” ventured Nikolai Vasilievich. “Kill them all, the dirty dogs!”
===Chinese===
我们俩也都没有香烟。

「我呢，」醉兵解释道，「你们知道，就是四处转悠，把『布尔茹伊』（bourgouys）们的肠子掏出来。」

「这就对了，同志，」尼古拉·瓦西里耶维奇壮着胆子搭话，「统统干掉，那些脏狗！」

===Original===
“I will,” said the soldier cheerily, and stalked off into the night, while we went our way.

Nikolai Vasilievich only shook his head and sighed and shook his head and sighed. He muttered something, but the wind that overtook us carried off his words. I could just catch “… my house﻿ ﻿… the mines.﻿ ﻿…”
===Chinese===
「我会的。」士兵喜滋滋地说，昂首阔步走进夜色里去了；我们呢，走我们自己的路。

尼古拉·瓦西里耶维奇只是摇着头叹气，又摇着头叹气。他咕哝了句什么，可是追上我们的那阵风把他的话刮跑了。我只隐约听得：「……我的房子……那些矿。……」

### IX / 九

===Original===
She was going along quickly, wrapped in the familiar fur; and it was snowing merrily.

“Nina!”

===Chinese===
她裹着那件熟悉的皮衣，走得很快；雪下得正欢。

「尼娜（Nina）！」

===Original===
She turned round and stopped, smiling. And the bright white winter day seemed to be smiling with her. It was the day of the Social Revolutionary coup d’état. Early in the morning troops of revolutionary partisans had occupied the city peacefully and taken possession of the public buildings, to wild cheering from the local crowds. The Russian national flag had been hauled down and a red one hoisted in its stead. Processions had appeared with revolutionary banners, and the town was decorated in red. “Have you heard the news?” she said. “Pàvel Pàvlovich, the Baron, has fled to Japan overnight, without telling us a word.”

“Of course, he was in danger of being arrested by the Reds,” I said. “But I suppose he’ll come back some day.”

===Chinese===
她回过头，站住，微笑着。明朗的白色冬日仿佛也跟着她一同微笑。这一天是社会革命党（Social Revolutionary）政变的日子。一清早，成队的革命游击队员和平地占领了全城，接管了公共建筑物，当地的人群向他们狂热欢呼。俄国国旗降了下来，升上去一面红旗取而代之。打着革命旗帜的游行队伍出现了，全城装点着一片红。「你听说消息了吗？」她说。「帕维尔·帕夫洛维奇（Pàvel Pàvlovich）——就是那位男爵——连夜逃到日本去了，一个字也没告诉我们。」

「他当然有被红军逮捕的危险，」我说。「不过我想，他总有一天会回来的。」

===Original===
She shook her head. “I don’t think so.”

“What does Sonia think?”

===Chinese===
她摇了摇头。「我看不会。」

「索妮娅（Sonia）怎么想？」

===Original===
“She’s glad.”

“*Glad?*”

===Chinese===
「她高兴着呢。」

「*高兴*？」

===Original===
“Yes. She was going to leave him herself﻿ ﻿… to marry Holdcroft. But now.﻿ ﻿…”

“Now what?”

===Chinese===
「是啊。她自己本来就打算离开他……好去嫁霍尔德克罗夫特（Holdcroft）。可是现在。……」

「现在怎么样？」

===Original===
“But now *he’s* left her.”

“Well, all the better, then. Saves trouble.”

“It’s﻿ ﻿… humiliating.”

===Chinese===
「可是现在是*他*离开了她。」

「唉，那倒更好。省了麻烦。」

「这……丢人。」

===Original===
We went on together and, nearing home, we cut through masses of new snow. It was one o’clock. The sun shone yellow. She put her hand into my coat pocket. Tender flecks, falling from the sky, would linger on her brows and lashes. We fumbled and wrangled in the snow; and, with that birdlike look of hers, she said, “Today﻿ ﻿… I like you.”

===Chinese===
我们一同往前走，快到家时，从一堆堆新雪中间穿过去。一点钟了。太阳黄澄澄地照着。她把手伸进我的大衣口袋。轻柔的雪片从天上落下来，赖在她的眉毛和睫毛上不肯走。我们在雪地里跌跌撞撞、推推搡搡；随后，她带着那种小鸟似的眼神说：「今天……我喜欢你。」

===Original===
At the American Headquarters dance last night she had been strangely, inexplicably hostile; and Fanny Ivanovna had made it worse by exhorting her to dance with me against her will. And, of course, there were Ward and White and Holdcroft. I remember sitting there that night with a sense of injury. What was the matter? Had I usurped too many of her dances? I felt as a man might feel who in a moment of particular goodwill towards mankind discovers that his watch has been pickpocketed. I said nothing, but strove to put it all into my look. She came up to me, rapturous, delicious. There was about her that night a disquieting, elusive charm. “I told you that I love you. What else do you want?” She said it with just that torturing proportion of smile and earnestness that you could not tell how it was meant: and very likely that was just how it was meant. I remember I ransacked my soul for something stinging. “You can’t love,” I said. “You’re not a woman; you’re a fish.” It is unfair to analyse love-reasoning unless in a similar emotional temperature. The dance over, our coats on, we sat and waited for the car, Nina looking rather sulky.﻿ ﻿… And today what a change the sunshine has wrought!

===Chinese===
昨晚在美军司令部的舞会上，她莫名其妙地、说不清道不明地满怀敌意；芬妮·伊万诺夫娜（Fanny Ivanovna）还苦苦劝她违着本心跟我跳舞，把事情弄得更糟。当然，还有沃德（Ward）、怀特（White）和霍尔德克罗夫特。我记得那天夜里我怀着满腹委屈坐在那儿。这是怎么回事？是我把她的舞霸占得太多了吗？我的感觉，就像一个人正对人类怀着一片特别的好意，却发现怀表叫人扒走了。我什么也没说，只极力把这一切都放进我的眼神里。她向我走来，如痴如醉，甜美可人。那天晚上，她身上有一种令人心神不宁、捉摸不定的魅力。「我告诉过你我爱你。你还想要什么？」她说这话时，笑意与正经配得恰是那么折磨人，叫你说不清这话是什么意思：而很可能，人家要的正是叫你说不清。我记得我在自己的灵魂里翻箱倒柜，想找一句扎人的话。「你不会爱，」我说。「你不是女人；你是条鱼。」不处在同样的感情温度里，就去分析恋爱的推理，未免有失公道。舞跳完了，大衣穿上了，我们坐着等汽车，尼娜显得相当愠恼。……而今天，阳光带来了多大的变化啊！

===Original===
We reached their house. “Come in,” she said.

“No.”

===Chinese===
我们到了她们家。「进来呀。」她说。

「不。」

===Original===
She went in, took off her coat, and while I lingered, came back and stood on the steps.

“You’ll catch cold like that.”

===Chinese===
她进去了，脱下大衣；趁我还磨蹭着没走，她又出来站在台阶上。

「你这样会着凉的。」

===Original===
She shook her head.

“I wish,” said I, “that women would propose to men.﻿ ﻿… I should love to say, ‘Oh, why can’t we remain just friends?’ ”

===Chinese===
她摇了摇头。

「我倒希望，」我说，「是女人向男人求婚。……那我就爱说一句：『哦，咱们为什么不能只做朋友呢？』」

===Original===
She looked at me. “You would say it to *me*?”

“Jokingly, of course.”

===Chinese===
她看着我。「你会对*我*说这话？」

「当然，是开玩笑。」

===Original===
“I shan’t propose then.”

“And if I said it seriously, would you propose then?”

===Chinese===
「那我就不求了。」

「要是我认真说呢，那你就求吗？」

===Original===
“Yes,” she laughed.

“Aren’t we supposed to be engaged, though?”

===Chinese===
「会的。」她笑了。

「不过话说回来，咱们不是算订了婚的吗？」

===Original===
“Are we?”

“I think so.”

===Chinese===
「算吗？」

「我看算。」

===Original===
“We’ll marry but divorce at once,” she said, “and live separately, and meet only once a year.”

===Chinese===
「咱们结了婚马上离婚，」她说，「然后各住各的，一年只见一次面。」

===Original===
And then the door opened and Nikolai Vasilievich said somewhat angrily to me: “Either come inside, or go. She’ll catch cold standing here with nothing on.” And as he vanished he rather slammed the door.

===Chinese===
正在这时，门开了，尼古拉·瓦西里耶维奇（Nikolai Vasilievich）有点气冲冲地对我说：「要么进来，要么走开。她什么也不穿站在这儿，要着凉的。」他身影刚一消失，门就砰的一声狠狠摔上了。

===Original===
“Go in, Nina, or he’ll be angry.”

“Take no notice of him. None of us take any notice of him. That’s why he is angry.”

===Chinese===
「进去吧，尼娜，不然他要生气了。」

「别理他。我们谁都不理他。所以他才生气。」

===Original===
“Then I’ll go in,” I said. And we both went in, and heard Fanny Ivanovna saying: “Believe me, Sonia, it’s all for the best. If you like, send him a postcard with ‘Good riddance’ on it. That’s all you need say.” And as I listened, it transpired further﻿—for misfortunes never come alone﻿—that Baron Wunderhausen was not a baron, and not even Wunderhausen.

===Chinese===
「那我就进去。」我说。我们两人进了屋，听见芬妮·伊万诺夫娜正在说：「相信我，索妮娅，这样反倒最好。你要是乐意，就给他寄张明信片，写上『走了才干净』。你要说的就这么多。」我再听下去，又听出一层——祸事从来不单行——温德豪森男爵（Baron Wunderhausen）根本不是男爵，甚至连温德豪森都不是。

===Original===
Sonia was downcast. “What the devil does it matter, anyhow,” argued Nikolai Vasilievich, “above all now that he is gone, whether he is a baron or no baron, Wunderhausen or no Wunderhausen?” But Sonia would not hear of it. That he should have left without telling her a word! That he should have lied to her all these years! Also she had always scoffed at him for his title, thought it ridiculous, almost a deliberate affectation. But now that the truth had been revealed to her and she knew that he had never had a title, she felt that she had been insulted rudely, married under false pretences. Well, she would insist on a divorce; she would take good care that she was the first in the field to insist on it. Holdcroft was extraordinarily attractive. He seemed rather keen on Vera, though. But how beautifully he danced.

===Chinese===
索妮娅垂头丧气。「这到底有什么了不得的，」尼古拉·瓦西里耶维奇争辩道，「何况他人已经走了，是男爵也罢、不是男爵也罢，是温德豪森也罢、不是温德豪森也罢，有什么要紧？」可索妮娅根本听不进去。他居然一个字不跟她说就走掉了！他居然这些年一直在骗她！再说，她原先还一直因为他那头衔讥笑他，觉得那头衔可笑得很，简直是一种存心装出来的派头。可如今真相大白，她知道他压根儿就不曾有过什么头衔，她只觉得自己受了粗暴的侮辱，是让人蒙骗着嫁了人。好，她非坚持离婚不可；她还要格外留神，务必自己抢在头里把这离婚提出来。霍尔德克罗夫特有说不出的魅力。不过，他好像对薇拉（Vera）挺上心。可他舞跳得多漂亮啊。

===Original===
And just that moment the gramophone, which Vera was fiddling with, broke loose into an intoxicating one-step. Nina, standing by it, echoed at the end of each refrain﻿—“My‑y‑y *cell*‑ar!” as the music galloped into syncopation.

===Chinese===
正在这个当口，薇拉摆弄着的那架留声机猛地撒开欢，奏出一支叫人陶醉的一步舞曲（one-step）。尼娜站在旁边，每逢叠句到尾便跟上一声——「我——呀——呀——*地*——窖！」——这时音乐正撒开蹄子奔进切分节奏里去。

===Original===
“Whose is the gramophone?”

It was Olya Olenin’s, the timid “football” little niece of Uncle Kostia.

===Chinese===
「这留声机是谁的？」

是奥莉娅·奥列宁（Olya Olenin）的，就是科斯佳叔叔（Uncle Kostia）那个怯生生的「足球」小侄女。

===Original===
“There they are!” cried Sonia. Three U.S. naval uniforms appeared in the window.

“If only we had more room here,” sighed Fanny Ivanovna. But how scrupulously clean she kept the little that there was of it.

===Chinese===
「他们来啦！」索妮娅喊道。窗口冒出三套美国海军制服。

「这儿地方再宽绰些就好了。」芬妮·伊万诺夫娜叹道。可就这么一点点地方，她却收拾得一尘不染、无可挑剔。

===Original===
“*I*’m forever *blow*‑ing bub‑*bles*,” hissed the gramophone.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===
「*我*永——远在*吹*泡——*泡*，」留声机嘶嘶地唱。……

===Original===
“*Fu*﻿—fu fu fu *fu*﻿—fu fu﻿—” whistled Nikolai Vasilievich. And, forgetful of her prodigal baronial spouse, Sonia dodged the chairs and sofa in the embrace of Holdcroft, while Kniaz sat in his corner seat, a little in the way, and read his paper and sucked sweets.

===Chinese===
「*夫*——夫夫夫*夫*——夫夫——」尼古拉·瓦西里耶维奇吹着口哨。索妮娅全然忘掉了她那位挥霍无度的男爵夫君，偎在霍尔德克罗夫特怀里绕着椅子沙发躲闪腾挪；公爵（Kniaz）坐在他那个角落座位上——多少有点碍事——看他的报，嘬他的糖。

===Original===
“You want to go?” Fanny Ivanovna looked at Nikolai Vasilievich with a solicitude that suggested a desire to anticipate his wishes. “All right. We’ll have our tea now. Sonia! Nina! Vera! Tea.”

“There’s no hurry,” he calmed her.

===Chinese===
「你想出去？」芬妮·伊万诺夫娜望着尼古拉·瓦西里耶维奇，那份关切里透着一股想抢先把他的心愿料到、替他办到的意思。「好吧。咱们这就喝茶。索妮娅！尼娜！薇拉！上茶。」

「不用急。」他安抚她道。

===Original===
During tea he was hilarious. He had been out in the streets and mixed with the crowds. What hilarious, happy crowds! The change had come about at last. Something would happen *now*. He said he thought it would be a few days only till the thing was finally settled. He meant to go and see some of the new ministers. A quite decent Government, it seemed; and what good order, all things considering. The Social-Revolutionaries had a double platform; they appealed to those who had no use for international militarism on revolutionary grounds, and to those who had no use for revolution on national grounds. And Nikolai Vasilievich thought that such broad-minded, reasonable people could not fail to see his point as regards the goldmines. I sat listening to him and in my influx of sudden happiness eating more than I really wanted to; for I felt she was *à moi* once more.

===Chinese===
喝茶的时候，他兴致极高。他上街去过了，跟人群混在一处。多么欢畅、多么快活的人群啊！变局总算来了。*如今*就要出事了。他说，他看只消几天功夫，事情就会彻底见分晓。他打算去拜见几位新部长。这届政府瞧着相当像样；通盘算来，秩序也实在不错。社会革命党人持的是双重纲领：一面以革命的理由去笼络那些不要国际军国主义的人，一面以民族的理由去笼络那些不要革命的人。而尼古拉·瓦西里耶维奇认为，这样胸怀宽大、通情达理的人，不可能看不出他在金矿问题上的道理。我坐着听他讲，一阵突如其来的幸福涌上来，吃得超过了本想吃的分量；因为我觉得她又*属于我*（à moi）了。

===Original===
He went out at last, and Fanny Ivanovna shut the door behind him. She looked at me, smiled, and then heaved a little sigh. “I let him do as he pleases,” she said. “Perhaps it’s better so. We’ll see.﻿ ﻿…”

===Chinese===
他终于出去了，芬妮·伊万诺夫娜在他身后把门关上。她望望我，微微一笑，随后轻轻叹了口气。「我随他去，」她说，「也许这样更好。走着瞧吧。……」

===Original===
As it darkened we took Olya home, and trailing our feet in the deep snow, carried the uncomfortably heavy gramophone, and marched in various formations, halted, marched again, and then, towards the climax, carried Nina in a burial procession. At the Olenins we danced again, I claiming Nina and the three American boys having to put up with what was “second best.” Madame Olenin, a suckling in a jumper at her breast, stood in the doorway and watched. A ten-years-old military cadet had followed her into the room and also stood in the doorway, in a civilian overcoat, and gaped at us. “Our Peter,” said she, “is a loyal little monarchist and refuses to take off his shoulder-straps in spite of the Red coup d’état.” The maternal hand stroked the offspring’s hair in a tender gesture. “But I made him put on this civilian overcoat on top. It isn’t safe, you know.”

===Chinese===
天擦黑的时候，我们送奥莉娅回家，脚在深雪里拖沓着，抬着那架沉得难受的留声机，一会儿排成这种队形、一会儿立定、一会儿又开步走，末了到了高潮处，还抬着尼娜走了一程出殡的行列。到了奥列宁家我们又跳起舞来，我独占了尼娜，三个美国小伙子只好领受「二流」的份儿。奥列宁夫人（Madame Olenin）怀里抱着个穿毛衫的奶娃娃，站在门口望着。一个十岁的士官生跟着她进了屋，也站在门口，身上罩一件平民大衣，张着嘴呆望我们。「咱们的彼得（Peter），」她说，「是个忠诚的小君主主义者，尽管发生了红色政变，也死活不肯摘下他的肩章。」做母亲的手温存地抚着孩子的头发。「不过我让他外头罩上这件平民大衣。您知道，这可不安全。」

===Original===
I came up and cuddled little Fanny in a rather inefficient fashion and lavished unmitigated praise, as is the classic way when talking to a mother of her babe. And then little Fanny, as is the classic way with babies, for no apparent cause, began howling, howling without rhyme or reason. I was made to play the piano, and I was pleasantly aware that Nina advertised me and showed me off as though I was her own special merchandise. The snow in the yard was pink from the sun as we jumped about on the sofa. She took water in her mouth and blew it out into my face, whereon I got her into a corner and slapped her hard, while the others looked on in amusement. She was trying to bite my hands; and then as we went out she *would* insist on fastening my overcoat.

===Chinese===
我走上前去，颇为笨手笨脚地搂了搂小芬妮，又滥施了一番毫不打折扣的赞美——这是同做母亲的谈论她娃娃时的经典路数。接着小芬妮也照婴儿的经典路数，无缘无故地嚎啕起来，没来由没道理地嚎个不停。大家又让我去弹钢琴；我愉快地觉察到，尼娜在替我作宣传、拿我炫耀，仿佛我是她一件特别的专卖品。我们在沙发上蹦跳的时候，院子里的雪被太阳映得粉红。她嘴里含了口水，噗地喷到我脸上；我便把她逼进一个角落，狠狠地拍她，其余的人饶有兴味地在旁看着。她一个劲儿想咬我的手；随后临出门，她*偏*非要给我扣上大衣不可。

===Original===
The others trailed behind, and we could hear their laughter growing fainter as we walked ahead. The snow creaked agreeably beneath our feet. It was five o’clock and there were the first signs of twilight. We passed the sombre silhouette of their little wooden house. Oh, how sad were these things in the winter.﻿ ﻿… Darkness was swiftly setting in. We crossed the wood. The tall pine-trees, covered with a thick coating of snow, stood mute and dreaming in the twilight; only their peaks moved ever so gently to and fro, murmuring some vague complaint.

===Chinese===
其他人拖拖拉拉跟在后面，我们走在前头，听得见他们的笑声越来越轻。雪在我们脚下发出悦耳的咯吱声。五点钟了，暮色初上。我们走过他们那座小木屋黑黢黢的轮廓。哦，这些东西在冬天里多么叫人伤心。……黑暗迅速地合拢来。我们穿过树林。高大的松树披着厚厚的积雪，在暮色里默默伫立，恍如梦中；只有树梢极其轻柔地来回摇摆，絮絮叨叨地诉着一段说不清的委屈。

===Original===
Then, suddenly, we came out into the open and saw the sea. Clad in an armour of ice, it was as smooth as a mirror. Here and there a monstrous snow-covered lump rose from the surface. The sky was grey and fretful and darkness fell upon us with every minute. The sun, as it set, slowly cast a feeble red flame on the sea chained in ice, and the crescent moon spread a yellow light over the surface, glimmering in varied colours on the ice, the snow, the glaciers. The wind strengthened and the frost pricked at my ears.

===Chinese===
随后，我们猛然走到空旷处，看见了海。海披着一副冰的铠甲，平滑如镜。东一处西一处，水面上升起一座座覆雪的庞然巨块。天空灰沉沉的，烦躁不安，黑暗一分钟一分钟地压到我们头上。西沉的太阳把微弱的红焰缓缓投在为冰封锁的大海上，一弯新月在海面铺开一片黄光，在冰上、雪上、冰川上闪出变幻不定的色彩。风紧起来，霜针刺着我的耳朵。

===Original===
“Say something! Say something!”

“What shall I say?”

===Chinese===
「说点什么呀！说点什么呀！」

「说什么呢？」

===Original===
“Why, you’re worse than Kniaz!” I exclaimed.

She smiled.

===Chinese===
「哎呀，你竟比公爵还不如！」我嚷道。

她微微一笑。

===Original===
“Say that the sea is a dazzling sight, that the moon is﻿ ﻿… well, anything you like, that the sun is red copper.”

===Chinese===
「你就说，这海真是耀眼好看，这月亮是……唉，随你怎么说吧，说这太阳是红铜。」

===Original===
She looked as though all this was nothing, but she alone was real. “Why falsify the tone? It’s there: I can see it.”

===Chinese===
她的神情，仿佛这一切都算不得什么，唯有她一个人是真实的。「何必歪曲这个调子？它就在那儿：我看得见。」

===Original===
“Is this not beautiful then? You’re an amazing creature! One doesn’t know which side to get hold of you. I talk to you about﻿ ﻿… about﻿ ﻿… *this*” (a florid gesture to the sea). “You tell me it is false.”

===Chinese===
「那么这难道不美吗？你真是个奇妙的家伙！人家都不知道该从哪一头亲近你。我跟你谈……谈……*这个*」（朝大海做了个浮夸的手势）「你却跟我说，这是虚假的。」

===Original===
“*This*” (an imitative florid gesture) “is all right. But please don’t talk about it to me.”

===Chinese===
「*这个*」（学了一个同样浮夸的手势）「倒是挺好。可是请别拿它来跟我谈。」

===Original===
She was silent.

“I liked you this morning,” she said then. “But *now*﻿—!”

===Chinese===
她不作声了。

「今天早上我是喜欢你的，」她随后说。「可是*现在*——！」

===Original===
“You see, the trouble is,” said I, “that you can’t talk of anything but foxtrots.”

===Chinese===
「你瞧，麻烦就在于，」我说，「你除了狐步舞，什么也谈不成。」

===Original===
“Last night at the American dance,” she said, “I danced with Ward.”

“I know, I saw you,” I said in a tone of condemnation.

===Chinese===
「昨晚在美国人的舞会上，」她说，「我同沃德跳了舞。」

「我知道，我看见了。」我用谴责的口吻说。

===Original===
“He’s very nice; I like him; but I can’t talk of anything to him. He asked me, ‘Do you like foxtrots?’ I said, ‘Yes.’ And when later on we danced the waltz, he said, ‘Do you like waltzes?’ And I said, ‘Yes.’ And he said. ‘I like them too.’ ”

===Chinese===
「他人很好，我也喜欢他；可是我跟他什么也谈不成。他问我：『你喜欢狐步舞吗？』我说：『喜欢。』后来我们跳华尔兹的时候，他问：『你喜欢华尔兹吗？』我说：『喜欢。』他说：『我也喜欢。』」

===Original===
“There you are!” I cried triumphantly. “You’ve got to stick to me and sack all the rest!”

===Chinese===
「这不结了！」我得意洋洋地喊道。「你就得守住我，把其余的人统统开除！」

===Original===
“You are nice,” she said, “and there are days when I like you﻿—though *you* never know when they are. But﻿ ﻿… I can’t talk to you.”

===Chinese===
「你是挺好的，」她说，「也有我喜欢你的日子——虽然*你*从来不知道是哪些日子。可是……我跟你谈不成。」

===Original===
And she added, “I am going home.”

===Chinese===
她又补了一句：「我要回家了。」

===Original===
The sun contracted and grew more red and feeble as the moon shone brighter and cast an even yellow light upon the space around us. Fretful fantastic shadows flitted across the ice. Objects about us grew black. Darkness was now hard upon us.

===Chinese===
太阳收拢了，变得更红、更弱；月亮却越发亮了，把一片均匀的黄光洒在我们四周的空间上。烦躁的、奇幻的影子掠过冰面。我们周围的物件都黑了下来。黑夜这时已经紧逼到我们头上了。

===Original===
We returned by moonlight that glimmered on the snow.

===Chinese===
我们踏着月光回去，月光在雪上莹莹地闪。

### V / 五

===Original===
When the train arrived at Omsk, the new regime of Kolchak had been established. The Admiral was distinctly pleased with the change; for he no longer believed in granting the Russian people a Constituent Assembly because he had grounds for thinking that the Russian people, if given this opportunity, would take advantage of it and elect a government other than that of Kolchak. And the Admiral was rather fond of little Kolchak, whose interpretation of democracy was that of denying the people the choice of government until such time as by some vague, mysterious, but anyhow protracted, system of education he hoped their choice would fall upon his own administration. We lived in our train, a verst or thereabouts from the station﻿—a thoroughly unwholesome place; and the Admiral diverted most of his time by throwing empty tobacco tins at the pigs that dwelt in the ditches around the train. “You have no conception what a pig a pig really is,” he said, “till you see an Omsk pig.”

===Chinese===
火车抵达鄂木斯克（Omsk）时，高尔察克（Kolchak）的新政权已经建立。海军上将对这一变故显然颇为满意；因为他已不再相信该让俄国人民拥有一届立宪会议了——他有充分的理由认为，俄国人民一旦得到这个机会，准会乘机用上一用，选出一个高尔察克以外的政府。何况海军上将还挺喜欢小个子的高尔察克，此君对民主的诠释是：先不许人民选择政府，直等到有朝一日——靠某种含糊其辞、神神秘秘、反正旷日持久的教育体系——他指望人民的选择会落到他本人的政府头上。我们住在自己的火车上，离车站一俄里（verst）上下——那地方极不利于健康；海军上将的大半时光，便靠朝那些栖居在车厢四周沟里的猪扔空烟草罐来打发光阴。「你要是没见过鄂木斯克的猪，」他说，「就无从想象猪究竟能猪到什么份上。」


===Original===
“Splendid!” said Sir Hugo. “Splendid!”

“There she goes again!” yelled the Admiral, and hit an old big sow with a Navy Cut tobacco tin.

===Chinese===
「妙极了！」雨果爵士（Sir Hugo）说。「妙极了！」

「瞧它又跑了！」海军上将在高叫，一只「Navy Cut」牌烟草罐掷中了一头老大的母猪。


===Original===
“Splendid effort!” said Sir Hugo. “Splendid effort!”

“I give dem h‑h‑hell!” roared General Bologoevski. “Damrotten pigs!” But, as usual, his threat remained an empty one.

===Chinese===
「掷得漂亮！」雨果爵士说。「掷得漂亮！」

「我给、给他们点厉害瞧瞧！」博洛戈耶夫斯基（Bologoevski）将军咆哮道。「烂、烂透的死猪！」然而一如既往，他的威胁仍旧是句空话。


===Original===
But while most of us were very much at sea as to why exactly we had arrived at Omsk, Nikolai Vasilievich seemed immune from doubt. Nikolai Vasilievich, suspicious of the punitive expedition, had arrived at the seat of the anti-Bolshevik Administration to seek redress and compensation in regard to his goldmines. I think it was chiefly for my British uniform that Nikolai Vasilievich asked me to accompany him on his visit to the General at the General Staff, before whom he was going to lay his case. I noticed that Nikolai Vasilievich had always had a curious habit of establishing some connection between his personal grievance and some powerful outside influence, as, for example, the general question of Allied intervention; and he insisted that he and we and intervention were really all one affair, and that hence a favourable solution of his financial difficulties was all part and parcel of that scheme which aimed at the defeat of Bolshevism.

===Chinese===
但是，我们多数人对于自己到底为什么到了鄂木斯克，实在如在五里雾中；尼古拉·瓦西里耶维奇却仿佛百疑不侵。尼古拉·瓦西里耶维奇疑心是讨伐队捣的鬼，于是来到这反布尔什维克政府的所在地，要为自己的金矿讨个昭雪、索赔一笔。我想，尼古拉·瓦西里耶维奇之所以要我陪着去见总参谋部（General Staff）的那位将军——他预备在这位将军面前陈述案情——多半是看中了我这身英国军装。我注意到，尼古拉·瓦西里耶维奇向来有个古怪的习惯，总要在他的私人冤屈同某种强有力的外来势力之间建立起联系，比如协约国干涉这一总问题；他一口咬定，他本人、我们大家、还有干涉，实在全是同一件事，因此他那桩财政困难的圆满解决，也正是那个旨在击溃布尔什维主义的整体计划中不可分割的一部分。


===Original===
We entered a large dirty waiting-room where crowds of petitioners awaited their turn with a patience that bordered on spiritual resignation: after the Russian manner they all desired to see the head man personally, whose life was consequently spent in interviews. A nasty dirty little woman with a nasty dirty little child, pointing at me with a dirty finger, was saying to her howling offspring, in an attempt to pacify her next-of-kin, “Is that your daddy, is he? Is that your daddy?”

===Chinese===
我们走进一间宽大而肮脏的候见室，一群群请愿者排着队等候轮到自己，那份耐心已近乎出世的听天由命：按俄国人的做派，人人都想亲自见到头号人物，于是头号人物的一生便尽耗在接见之中了。一个邋遢肮脏的小女人，带着个邋遢肮脏的小娃娃，正用一根脏手指头指着我，一心要安抚她那嚎哭的至亲骨肉：「那是你爸爸，是不是呀？那是你爸爸？」


===Original===
The General was an elusive person, a wily man, a master in the art of compromise. He was the idol of the Allies. He was one of those few who could so wangle things, so balance favours, as to please at once all the multitudinous Allies and even curry favour with a large majority of Russians. His habitual procedure was this. If an Ally asked him, for example, for the allotment of a certain building, he always promised without reserve. Then the Russian organization in possession of that building would at once cry out in protest; and he immediately assured them that they would be allowed to keep the building: the whole matter, he explained, was a mere misunderstanding. Then the Russian organization stayed, and when the Ally came to take the building over they referred the Ally to the General. And when the Ally came to him and asked for explanation, the General, with a charming smile, would say, “Well, you see that building is not really suitable for your use. I will find you a better one.” Then the Ally waited. He must have time, the General said; and actually he played on time, on “evolution.” And in the meantime there was a coup d’état; or the Russian organization went bankrupt; or the particular Allied representative who had been worrying him was replaced by another, with whom the General would begin again at the beginning; or the Allied troops were about to be withdrawn; or the city was recaptured by the Sovets; or there was a fire and the correspondence was buried in the flames. He was a man who had no use whatever for “free will” and played entirely on “predestination.”

===Chinese===
这位将军是个难以捉摸的人物，一个狡黠的老手，妥协艺术的大师。他是协约国的偶像。天下能把事情这样蒙混摆布、把人情这样左右权衡，一下子讨得芸芸众协约国个个欢心、甚至还能笼络住绝大多数俄国人的，寥寥可数，他便是其中之一。他的惯用章程如下：譬如某协约国请他拨一所房子，他总是毫无保留地一口应承。于是占用那所房子的俄国机构立刻嚷嚷抗议；他便马上向他们担保，这房子照旧归他们用：整个这件事，他解释说，纯属误会。于是俄国机构照住不误；等协约国来接管房子，他们便把协约国指点到将军那里去。等协约国到他跟前讨个解释，将军便带着迷人的微笑说：「唉，您瞧，那所房子其实并不合您使用。我给您找一所更好的。」于是协约国便等。总得给他点时间，将军说；实际上，他玩的就是时间，玩的就是「演变」。而这期间，不是政变发生了；就是那家俄国机构破了产；再不然，就是那个搅得他不宁的协约国代表换了人，将军便同新来的再从头开始；要么协约国军队眼看要撤；要么城市被苏维埃（Sovets）重新攻克；要么一场大火，把往来公文统统葬入火海。这个人对「自由意志」毫无用处，玩的全是「宿命」。


===Original===
The General listened to Nikolai Vasilievich’s emotional narrative in a friendly manner, and smiling pleasantly he rose and shook hands, as if to show that the interview was at an end, saying, “You may rest assured that it will be quite all right. Call again one of these days.”

Nikolai Vasilievich went out, beaming. “Well,” he said, “it seems settled.” I tendered my heartiest congratulations.

===Chinese===
将军友好地听完了尼古拉·瓦西里耶维奇声情并茂的叙述，随即满面愉快地起身握手，仿佛以此表明接见到此为止，口中说道：「您尽管放心，一切都会妥妥当当的。过些日子再来一趟吧。」

尼古拉·瓦西里耶维奇满面红光地走了出来。「喏，」他说，「看来是定下来了。」我向他致以最衷心的祝贺。


===Original===
Then “one of these days” we called upon the General a second time. Nikolai Vasilievich laid great stress on the dastardly action of the Czechs﻿—that nation just then being out of court with the government at Omsk﻿—but the General merely said, “Wait till the Supreme Ruler returns from Perm. I can do nothing without the Supreme Ruler.”

===Chinese===
于是，到了「过些日子」，我们第二次拜访将军。尼古拉·瓦西里耶维奇着重陈说捷克人（Czechs）的卑劣行径——那个民族当时正失宠于鄂木斯克政府——将军却只说：「等最高执政（Supreme Ruler）从彼尔姆（Perm）回来再说。没有最高执政，我什么也办不成。」


===Original===
Nikolai Vasilievich then waited for the return of the Supreme Ruler; and presently we called again. The General’s manner, as he received us, was considerably less sunny than it had been on the two previous occasions. “You have been here before,” he greeted Nikolai Vasilievich. “You must have patience and wait.”

“*Wait?*” asked Nikolai Vasilievich in a tone of secret terror, the terror of a man who had been doing naught else all his life﻿—and knew its meaning.

===Chinese===
于是尼古拉·瓦西里耶维奇便等最高执政回来；不久我们再度登门。将军接待我们时的那副态度，比起前两回来，可就逊色得多了。「您来过了。」他招呼尼古拉·瓦西里耶维奇道。「您得有耐心，等着。」

「*等*？」尼古拉·瓦西里耶维奇问，声音里含着一种隐秘的恐怖——那是一个一辈子没干过别的、专事等待的人的恐怖——他深知此中三昧。


===Original===
“Yes, I advise you to wait. Have patience.”

“How long?” asked Nikolai Vasilievich.

“How do I know?” the General replied. “Wait﻿—and you will see.”

===Chinese===
「是啊，我劝您等。要有耐心。」

「等多久？」尼古拉·瓦西里耶维奇问。

「我哪儿知道？」将军答道。「等着——自见分晓。」


===Original===
Now, was it that Nikolai Vasilievich had waited long enough and seen nothing? Was it that in the circumstances he thought it sounded too much like a mockery? Or was it the explosion of that brewing restlessness that he had gathered in the years of intermittent waiting: the last puff of ineffectual remonstrance before his final sinking into hopeless resignation? But suddenly Nikolai Vasilievich went wild. I had never seen him in that state before. He abused the General in immoderate terms. He accused him first of turning honest people into Bolsheviks; then of being in the pay of Moscow. He threatened to lead a rebellion against the Kolchak State. Nikolai Vasilievich ceased to be a man and became an incarnation: Man having lost his patience: Humanity gone wild in the waiting. He thundered forth at the adversary, and his ruined hopes were the woes of Humankind. Then, coming to the end of his intellectual resources, but far from having yet exhausted his spiritual wrath, he made reference to the Day of Judgment. The door into the chancery flew open, and the Chief of Staff, the Aide-de-camp, and heads of various departments dashed upon the scene, wondering what on earth had happened; and shouting loudly Nikolai Vasilievich hurled abuse upon the Chief-of-Staff, the Aide-de-camp, and the heads of various departments. And then in the waiting-room he went for a stray Admiral, a petitioner like himself, and hurled abuse at him as well.

===Chinese===
那么，是尼古拉·瓦西里耶维奇等得够久了、却什么也没等着吗？是他觉得处在此情此景，这话听上去未免太像嘲弄了吗？还是他多年断断续续的等待里积攒下的那股酝酿中的躁郁，此刻来了个总爆发：那是他终于沉入绝望的听天由命之前，最后一口徒劳抗议的喘息？总之，尼古拉·瓦西里耶维奇突然发狂了。我从没见过他这副模样。他破口痛骂将军，用字毫无节制。他先是指控他把老实人通通变成了布尔什维克；继而指控他领着莫斯科的津贴。他扬言要率众造反，反抗高尔察克的国家。尼古拉·瓦西里耶维奇不复是一个人，而成了一尊化身：失了耐心的人：在等待中发狂的人类。他向着对头雷霆般怒喝，他那破灭的希望便是全人类的灾殃。随后，智慧的库存告罄——心头之怒却远未泄尽——他便援引起最后审判日（Day of Judgment）来。通办公室的门砰地弹开，参谋长（Chief of Staff）、副官（Aide-de-camp）和各部门的首脑一拥而出，不知究竟出了什么事；尼古拉·瓦西里耶维奇高声叫骂，朝着参谋长、副官和各部门首脑痛骂不止。接着到了候见室，他又冲着一位不知打哪儿冒出来的海军上将——一位同他一样的请愿者——也照样骂了一通。


===Original===
“All right,” the General said at length. “All right. If you won’t be reasonable, I shall have to resort to the recognized procedure. Guard!” And he ordered them to take Nikolai Vasilievich away. Nikolai Vasilievich still raged and fluttered, and the guards came up to him with signs of deference and indecision. “Come on, sir,” they persuaded him, “he really means it.” And taking him each under one arm, they dragged him out into the open.

===Chinese===
「好了，」将军终于开口。「好了。您要是不讲道理，我就只得诉诸规定程序了。卫兵！」他随即下令把尼古拉·瓦西里耶维奇带走。尼古拉·瓦西里耶维奇兀自咆哮扑腾，卫兵们走上前去，一脸又恭敬又拿不定主意的样子。「走吧，先生，」他们劝他，「他这回可是当真的。」随后一人一边架住他的胳膊，把他拖到了露天里。


===Original===
We walked back to the train.

“What those people will not realize,” I took it up to humour him, “is that you can’t live on nothing. Waiting doesn’t feed you, and waiting doesn’t clothe you; and when you have a family﻿—”

===Chinese===
我们步行回火车去。

「那些人不肯明白的是，」我接过话头，顺着哄他，「人不能靠喝西北风活着。等待不能当饭吃，等待不能当衣穿；再说，一个人要是还有家小——」


===Original===
“Of course, one can borrow,” said Nikolai Vasilievich.

“Yes, of course,” I agreed.

===Chinese===
「当然，还可以借嘛。」尼古拉·瓦西里耶维奇说。

「是啊，当然。」我附和道。


===Original===
Fanny Ivanovna greeted him with “Well, Nikolai, is it all arranged?”

A fiendish look came on his face, as though he said, “The hell it is!” and all the more fiendish because he did not say it.

===Chinese===
芬妮·伊万诺夫娜迎着他便问：「喏，尼古拉，都安排妥当了吗？」

他脸上现出一副凶相，仿佛在说：「妥个鬼！」而且正因为他没有说出口，那副凶相便愈发凶了。


===Original===
She sighed conspicuously. And her sigh gave him a nervous shudder. A look of hate came into his steel-grey eyes. “She even sighs offensively,” he said to me, “as though she meant to charge me with the necessity of doing so.”

===Chinese===
她引人注目地叹了口气。这一叹，叫他不免神经质地一颤。他那双铁灰色的眼睛里浮起憎恨的神色。「她连叹气都带着冒犯，」他对我说，「仿佛存心认定这口气非叹不可，而这笔账须记在我名下。」


===Original===
“Nikolai!” she cried, “don’t let yourself go before strangers. What will Andrei Andreiech think of you! You know I am not to blame because the mines won’t pay. And you ought to remember that I advised you to sell them long ago, and if you had listened to me then we shouldn’t have been in this plight. Well, well, it’s no use quarrelling now. We’ve got to wait, that’s all.”

===Chinese===
「尼古拉！」她喊道，「当着外人的面，可别由着性子撒开来。安德烈·安德烈伊奇要怎么看你呢！你知道，矿不肯出钱，怪不得我。你也该记得，我早就劝你把矿卖掉，当时你要是听了我的话，咱们如今何至于落到这步田地。唉，唉，如今吵架也没用了。咱们只好等，仅此而已。」


===Original===
The ironic fascination of the situation at this point proved irresistible. “There’s an English proverb,” I supplied: “ ‘All things come to him who waits.’ ”

===Chinese===
到了这一步，这局面中的反讽魅力实在叫人抵挡不住。「英国有句谚语，」我凑上一句：「『凡是等待的人，终会得到一切。』」


===Original===
“Hm!” said Nikolai Vasilievich.

“And there’s another one: ‘Rome wasn’t built in a day.’ ”

“Excellent proverbs!” he said dryly.

===Chinese===
「哼！」尼古拉·瓦西里耶维奇说。

「还有一句：『罗马不是一天建成的。』」

「好极了的谚语！」他干巴巴地说。


===Original===
Kniaz popped his head out from behind the paper, like a mouse, and added, “There’s our own Russian proverb, too: ‘The slower you drive the farther you get.’ ”

“You, Kniaz, had better read your paper,” retorted Nikolai Vasilievich acidly. “What does it say in there?”

===Chinese===
公爵从报纸后面探出头来，活像只耗子，也跟着补上一句：「咱们俄国也有自己的谚语：『赶路赶得越慢，走得反倒越远。』」

「你呀，公爵，还是看你的报纸吧，」尼古拉·瓦西里耶维奇尖酸地抢白道。「报上都说了些什么？」


===Original===
I stood at the window of the stationary train and watched the sinking landscape dissolve in the gathering gloom about us. Why did the winter air seem so acutely strange, as if charged with something, a kind of tenderness, a warm, transfiguring love﻿ ﻿… ? Nikolai Vasilievich came to my side and watched, his hands in his trouser pockets.

===Chinese===
我站在那列停驻不动的火车的窗前，望着渐渐沉没的景色在我们四周聚拢的昏暝里消融。为什么冬日的空气显得这般尖锐地异样，仿佛饱含着什么——一种柔情，一种温暖的、令人焕然一新的爱……？尼古拉·瓦西里耶维奇踱到我身旁，双手插在裤袋里望着。


===Original===
“Pigs in the ditches,” he brooded, “pigs in offices, everywhere.﻿ ﻿… A town of pigs. That General﻿ ﻿… oh! what a pig.﻿ ﻿…”

===Chinese===
「沟里是猪，」他幽幽地自语，「办公室里也是猪，到处都是……一座猪的城。那位将军……哦！好一头猪……」

### VI / 六

===Original===
The “affiliation” of Eisenstein into our “society” was a tribute to his own unflagging perseverance. It so happened that while in Vladivostok the Admiral had been in urgent need of a dentist, and quite by accident he tumbled against Eisenstein, who had set up a practice there. The Admiral, though he loathed all Jews, was yet favourably impressed by Eisenstein because on his first visit to him he heard Eisenstein engage in a vigorous cursing of his Chinese servant. He liked to see a man who knew how to put “these people” in their places, a man who knew how to assert his own authority, a man who did not talk about “equality” and suchlike tosh (discordant with his sentiment), “utopia,” “socialism,” and that sort of thing, you know, that has made the world, etc. etc. There was altogether too much Bolshevism abroad, and the vigorous action of the dentist with his Chink appealed to him unspeakably.

“This clamouring for allowing men from below to come up to the top and not imposing individuals of the old governing class from above,” he said. “All damned well to talk like that, but in the meantime is anarchy to be allowed to continue unchecked? Apparently so.”

“Orright! Orright!” said Eisenstein.

===Chinese===
爱森斯坦（Eisenstein）得以「加盟」我们这个「社交圈」，不能不归功于他自己那份锲而不舍的坚忍。事情是这样的：海军上将当时在符拉迪沃斯托克（Vladivostok），急等着寻一位牙医，纯属偶然，撞上了在那儿开业行医的爱森斯坦。海军上将虽然厌恶一切犹太人，对爱森斯坦却颇有好感，因为头一回登门，便听见爱森斯坦正起劲地痛骂他的中国仆人。他就爱看这种男人：懂得怎样把「这号人」收拾得服服帖帖，懂得怎样立起自己的威风，不谈「平等」之类的废话（与他心气不投），不谈「乌托邦」啦、「社会主义」啦那一套——你知道，就是那号把世界搅和成今天这般模样的劳什子，等等，等等。世上布尔什维主义本来就闹得太凶，而这位牙医整治中国佬的凌厉手段，更让他说不出的受用。

「如今又吵吵嚷嚷，说什么要容许底下的人升到顶上去，不要再从顶上把旧统治阶级的人物强派下来，」他说。「这么讲讲固然痛快，可是这当口，难道就任凭无政府状态毫无节制地继续下去么？看来正是如此。」

「奥赖特！奥赖特！」（Orright! Orright!）爱森斯坦应道。

===Original===
This seemed the only word he knew in English. But it did not baffle him in the least; indeed he preferred to converse in English by means of its continual solitary use to any reasonable conversation in Russian; and when the Admiral spoke Russian to him he still replied, “Orright! Orright!” The Admiral had found him an amazing dentist. The Admiral’s teeth and dentistry seemed the subject he was least interested in of all. He talked politics and finance. At intervals strange men and women of a strong Hebrew strain would run into the room, and Eisenstein, leaving the Admiral with his mouth wide open and cotton-wool stuck under his tongue, would exchange queries in a quick and agitated manner with these dark intruders. The Admiral would hear such phrases as “What is the yen today? How much is the dollar?” And if the Admiral chanced to touch the question of finance, Eisenstein would pounce upon him with inquiries: “Do you want dollars? How many dollars? Or can I sell you francs?” Or suddenly he would ask the Admiral to recommend his being made a British subject. Where was the difficulty? He could always change his name Eisenstein to Ironstone, which, he believed, sounded jolly well in English.

===Chinese===
